Primitive Feynman diagrams and the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links
Bruno Dular

Abstract
. Goussarov–Habiro’s theory of clasper surgeries defines a filtration of the monoid of string links L ⁡ ( m ) L(m) on m m strands, in a way that geometrically realizes the Feynman diagrams appearing in low-dimensional and quantum topology. Concretely, L ⁡ ( m ) L(m) is filtered by C n C_{n} -equivalence, for n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , which is defined via local moves that can be seen as higher crossing changes. The graded object associated to the Goussarov–Habiro filtration is the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links ℒ ​ L ​ ( m ) \mathcal{L}L(m) . We give a concrete presentation, in terms of primitive Feynman (tree) diagrams and relations ( 1T ⁡ T \1T , AS \AS , IHX \IHX , STU 2 \STU^{2} ), of the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra ℒ ​ L ​ ( m ) ℚ \mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}} . To that end, we investigate cycles in graphs of forests : flip graphs associated to forest diagrams and their STU \STU relations. As an application, we give an alternative diagrammatic proof of Massuyeau’s rational version of the Goussarov–Habiro conjecture for string links, which relates indistinguishability under finite type invariants of degree < n <n and C n C_{n} -equivalence.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background

A
string link on
m
strands in the cylinder
is a smooth embedding of a disjoint union of
m
intervals into the cylinder

\gamma\colon\{1,\dots,m\}\times[0,1]\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{D}^{2}\times[0,1]

such that
\gamma(i,e)=(x_{i},e)
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
e\in\{0,1\}
, where
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in\mathbb{D}^{2}
are fixed points
1
1

1

The points
x_{i}
are usually ordered along the
x
-axis.
, and
\gamma
is perpendicular to the boundary near those points
2
2

2

This last condition ensures that concatenation of string links is well-defined, i.e. the concatenation remains a smooth embedding.
. String links on
m
strands are considered up to smooth isotopy
3
3

3

One can replace isotopies by
ambient isotopies
by the
smooth isotopy extension theorem
.
, and
L(m)
denotes the corresponding set of isotopy classes. When there is no ambiguity, elements of
L(m)
are also called
string links
.

The set
L(m)
is a
monoid
with multiplication given by vertical concatenation (Figure
1(b)
), with the trivial string link as unit (Figure
1(c)
).

(a)

A knot and a long knot

(b)

Two string links and their concatenation in
L(3)

(c)

Trivial string link in
L(3)

Figure 1
.
Long knots and string links

When
m=1
, elements of
L(1)
are called
long knots
and
closing a long knot
yields an isomorphism between
L(1)
and the monoid of knots under connected sum (Figure
1(a)
). Thus, the study and classification of string links naturally belong to knot theory, and one of the main tools for probing them is the use of
invariants
.

A string link invariant
V\colon L(m)\rightarrow A
valued in an abelian group
A
is a
Vassiliev invariant
or
finite type invariant
of
degree
n
if it satisfies specific
skein relations

[
Vas90
,
BL93
,
Bar95
]
, which can be read as the condition that
V
vanishes on the
(n+1)
-st power of the augmentation ideal in the monoid ring
\mathbb{Z}L(m)
. Vassiliev invariants dominate
4
4

4

Here,
dominate
means that the collection of all Vassiliev invariants determines the mentioned known invariants.
polynomial invariants (Conway, HOMFLY, etc.), quantum invariants
[
Tur16
]
, Milnor invariants
[
Mei21
]
, etc. but it is an open question whether they separate string links (or even knots, in the case
m=1
). One of the main results in that theory, due to Kontsevich, is that any
\mathbb{Q}
-valued Vassiliev invariant
V
factors through the Kontsevich integral

(1.1)

Z\colon L(m)\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}

which is valued in the graded completion of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
Feynman diagrams
on
m
strands
[
Kon93
,
Bar95
]
, generated by uni-trivalent diagrams (with cyclic orientation at the trivalent vertices
5
5

5

The cyclic orientation is usually not indicated, in which case it is assumed to be counterclockwise.
, and univalent vertices attached to
m
vertical strands, see Figure
5
), modulo
\1T
and
\STU
relations (which imply the relations
\4T,\AS,\IHX
, see Figures
2
,
3
). The presence of the
\1T
relation translates the fact that we are working with
unframed string links
. This justifies the notation
\FI
for
framing independence
, used throughout this paper.

For the reason above, the Kontsevich integral is called a
universal Vassiliev invariant over
\mathbb{Q}
. The theory is much less understood over the integers.

(a)

A
\4T
,
four-terms
, relation

(b)

\STU
relations

(c)

A
\1T
,
one-term
, relation

Figure 2
.
The
\4T
,
\STU
and
\1T
relations

(a)

An
\AS
relation

(b)

An
\IHX
relation in the
Kirchhoff form

[
CDM12
, Section 5.2.7]

Figure 3
.
The
\AS
and
\IHX
relations

A natural question about Vassiliev invariants is: what do they detect? In the case of knots (
m=1
), Goussarov
[
Gou98
]
and Habiro
[
Hab00
]
independently answered this question by introducing
C_{n}
-moves
, defined using
clasper surgeries
. Those are local moves on string links that are modelled on
tree claspers
, ribbon trees with leaves attached to the strands, that geometrically realize the Feynman diagrams from above. See Figure
4
for examples of
C_{1}
- and
C_{2}
-moves. More precisely,
C_{n}
-moves generate the
C_{n}
-equivalence relation on
L(m)
, and they show that any two
C_{n}
-equivalent string links are
V_{n}
-equivalent, i.e. they cannot be distinguished by Vassiliev invariants of degree
<n
. They conjecture that the converse holds as well: this is the
Goussarov–Habiro conjecture for string links in the cylinder
(which they prove for knots, i.e. the case
m=1
). This is still wide open, with only progress made in small degrees
[
MY10
]
.

(a)

A
C_{1}
-move

(b)

A
C_{2}
-move

Figure 4
.
Diagrams, claspers realizing them and
C_{n}
-moves

For each
n\geq 1
the quotient monoid
L(m)/C_{n+1}
turns out to be a nilpotent group, and
C_{n+1}
-equivalence classes of
C_{n}
-trivial string links form a finitely generated abelian group
\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)\coloneqq L_{n}(m)/C_{n+1}

[
GPV00
,
Hab00
]
. Those combine into a graded Lie
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra
\mathcal{L}L(m)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)
, the
Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links on
m
strands
. From their work, it follows that there is a surjective
\mathbb{Z}
-linear graded
realization map

R\colon\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)
that sends a tree diagram to the result of surgery along a tree clasper realizing it. Thus there is a presentation of the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra by tree diagrams on
m
strands modulo specific relations

\mathcal{L}L(m)\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)/\ker(R),

whose understanding would be a significant step towards the Goussarov--Habiro conjecture
6
6

6

The Goussarov–Habiro conjecture is equivalent to injectivity of the
comparison map

\chi\colon\mathcal{L}L(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{A}L(m)\colon[\gamma]\mapsto[\gamma-1]
, where
\mathcal{A}L(m)
is the graded algebra associated with the Vassiliev filtration of the monoid ring
\mathbb{Z}L(m)
.
, while also being of its own interest. For example, it would provide a “
universal Goussarov–Habiro invariant
”, valued in a Lie algebra of tree diagrams. This is related to the study of
\pi_{0}
of the embedding calculus tower
[
Con08
,
Bud+17
,
Kos20
]
. The concordance analogous of this question, where
C_{n}
-equivalence is replaced by
C_{n}
-concordance
appears in works of Conant, Schneiderman and Teichner
[
CST12
,
CST16
]
, while the homotopy analogous has been settled by Habegger, Lin and Masbaum
[
HL90
,
HM00
]
. Habiro and Massuyeau studied a similar question in the context of homology cylinders in
[
HM09
]
. Nozaki, Sato and Suzuki also studied
\ker(R)
in
[
NSS22
]
.

1.2.
Results and strategy

1.2.1.
Main result

The main contribution of the present paper is an explicit presentation of the
rational
Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\mathcal{L}L(m)\otimes\mathbb{Q}
. In other words, we identify
\ker(R)_{\mathbb{Q}}
as the subgroup generated by specific relations in
\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
: the usual
\1T,\AS,\IHX
relations, completed with Conant’s
\STU^{2}
relation, see (
1.4
). We also prove that this algebra of tree diagrams is the primitive Lie algebra of the Hopf algebra of Feynman diagrams
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Theorem 1

(Presentation of the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links)
.

There is an isomorphism of Lie
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras

\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

defined by realizing tree diagrams using clasper surgeries, whose inverse is induced by the Kontsevich integral.

The strategy of the proof is outlined below. It follows from Theorems
2.4.5.1
,
3.1.4.1
and
3.2.2.1
in the text.

1.2.2.
Strategy and side results

For identifying the kernel of the realization map
R
, which takes tree diagrams to classes of string links, it is natural to look for a map in the other direction. This is exactly what the
Kontsevich integral
(
1.1
) does
[
Kon93
,
Bar95
]
. Moreoever, it detects tree clasper surgeries, in a sense made precise by the following result
7
7

7

See
[
Oht02
, Proposition E.24]
for a similar result in the context of the
LMO invariant
.
.

Theorem 2

(Tree preservation theorem (Theorem
3.2.2.1
))
.

Let
n\geq 1
and
T\in\mathcal{D}^{T}_{n}(m)
a degree
n
tree diagram on
m
strands. Then
8
8

8

The use of the big-
O
notation indicates that this is an equality modulo terms of degree
\geq n+1
.

(1.2)

Z(\sigma(C_{T}))=1+T+O(n+1),

where
\sigma(C_{T})
denotes the string link obtained from the trivial one by surgery along a tree clasper
C_{T}
realizing
T
, and
O(n+1)
means that the equality holds modulo terms of degree
\geq n+1
.

Consequently, the Kontsevich integral induces a map
Z^{GH}\colon\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, defined for
[\gamma]\in\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)
as
Z^{GH}([\gamma])\coloneqq Z_{n}(\gamma)
, whose image lies in the subspace generated by tree diagrams
\vspan_{\mathbb{Q}}(\text{trees})\subset\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. As in the case of knots, i.e.
m=1
, the subspace generated by tree diagams is precisely the primitive Lie algebra of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
(Theorem
2.2.2.3
in the text, see also (
2.9
) and Definition
2.2.2.1
):

Theorem 3

(Primitive versus size filtration (Theorem
2.2.2.3
))
.

The reduced primitive filtration and the size filtration of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
coincide. In particular,
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is the submodule of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
generated by tree diagrams.

As its name suggests, the
size filtration

F^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
filters
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
by the number of connected components of diagrams, i.e.
F^{k}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is generated by forest diagrams with
\leq k
trees. The main ingredient of the proof of Theorem
3
requires averaging over permutations, which is the reason why we cannot extend Theorem
3
over
\mathbb{Z}
with the current methods.

So far, the situation is summarized by the following commutative diagram:

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{GH}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}}

and the kernel of
\tilde{\iota}
contains the
\1T,\AS,\IHX
relations. It is a standard application of clasper calculus that the realization map
R
satisfies those relations. But since
\STU
does not preserve the size (or the primitive) filtration, it is not clear what other relations hold in
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
, hence what other relations
R
may satisfy.

Actually, over
\mathbb{Q}
a single additional type of relation is needed: Conant’s
\STU^{2}
relation
[
Con08
]

(1.4)

where the four terms are tree (or forest) diagrams identical outside of the shown parts. As for the
\STU
relation, we sometimes write
\STU^{2}
as
F^{=Y}-F^{\times Y}=F^{Y=}-F^{Y\times}
. This relation preserves the size filtration.

Through the introduction of
graph of forests
, we describe a concrete presentation by generators and relations of the primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
(Theorem
2.4.5.1
in the text). The main ideas of the proof are outlined at the beginning of Section
2
. This constitutes the main technical result of this paper.

Theorem 4

(Primitive Lie algebra of trees (Theorem
2.4.5.1
))
.

The primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is naturally
9
9

9

Here,
naturally
means that the isomorphism is induced by the map sending a diagram to itself.
isomorphic to the graded
Lie algebra of trees

(1.5)

\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}}

endowed with the bracket
[T,T^{\prime}]\coloneqq\overrightarrow{(T^{\prime}T)(TT^{\prime})}
for any
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
and extended linearly
10
10

10

See Definition
2.3.3.6
for the
\overrightarrow{(\cdot)}
notation.
.

In the course of proving the above theorem, we actually obtain a concrete presentation of the primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
as well. For
k\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)\subset\mathcal{D}^{F}(m)
be the subset of size
k
forest diagrams. A new relation is necessary:

Definition
.

A
\hexagon
-relation
is a sum of forest diagrams of the form

(1.6)

where all terms are identical forest diagrams outside of the part shown.

Like
\STU^{2}
, the
\hexagon
-relation preserves the size filtration and
\hexagon R\subset\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}(m)
denotes the submodule generated by all
\hexagon
-relations. We obtain the following result, which is Theorem
2.4.4.1
:

Theorem 5

(Presentation of the primitive filtration (Theorem
2.4.4.1
))
.

For all
k\geq 1
, the
graded
\mathbb{Q}
-module of size
k
forests

(1.7)

\mathcal{F}^{\FI,k}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.49501pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.24501pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.24501pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.45833pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}}

is naturally isomorphic to the
k
-th step of the reduced primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. In particular, there is an isomorphism of filtrations

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}^{\FI,1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}^{\FI,2}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\subset\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P^{1}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P^{2}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\subset\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

where the bottom line is the reduced primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Moreover, the vertical isomorphisms preserve the grading.

Remark 1.2.2.1
.

In degree
n
and size
n
,
\STU^{2}
becomes
\4T
and
\hexagon R
is trivial. In degree
n
and size
1
,
\hexagon R
is trivial. In degree
n
and size
<n-1
,
\hexagon R
follows from
\STU^{2}
and
\IHX
.
∎

Lastly, the above results combine into a concrete presentation of the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra, as mentioned at the beginning of this section:

Theorem 6

(Presentation of the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra)
.

There is an isomorphism of graded Lie
\mathbb{Q}
-algebras

(1.8)

R\colon\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

that sends a tree diagram
T
to the class
R(T)
of the string link obtained by performing a clasper surgery along a tree clasper
C_{T}
whose underlying diagram is
T
. By the tree preservation theorem, the inverse of
R
is induced by the Kontsevich integral and denoted
Z^{GH}
.

The commutative diagram
1.3
can then be completed into

(1.9)

where:
\mathcal{A}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the Hopf algebra of string links associated to finite-type invariants,
R
is an isomorphism by Theorem
6
,
R^{v}
is an isomorphism by Kontsevich’s theorem, and
\iota
is an isomorphism by Theorem
4
. Consequently, this implies that the comparison
\chi
is injective over
\mathbb{Q}
. This yields an alternative proof of the rational Goussarov–Habiro conjecture, which had already been shown by Massuyeau
[
Mas07
]
using completely different methods. This is explicited in Section
4
.

1.3.
Further directions

1.3.1.
Towards the integral story

In this work, we had to work over the rationals for two main reasons: the averaging over permutations and the use of the Kontsevich integral. However, many things still work over
\mathbb{Z}
: one can define an adequate Lie
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra of tree diagrams and it surjects onto the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}L(m)
as in Theorem
3.1.4.1
. We plan on investigating new relations in this Lie
\mathbb{Z}
-algebra, and its comparison with the concordance version of the theory
[
CST16
]
in future work.

1.3.2.
Framed string links

In this paper, we restricted our attention to unframed string links in order to make the presentation clearer. However, the main algebraic ingredient (the identification of the primitive Lie algebra of
\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
) holds with and without the
\1T
relation. By considering the Kontsevich integral for
framed string links

[
LM96
]
, one should obtain a presentation of the framed version of the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra.

1.3.3.
Embedding calculus approach

We use the Kontsevich integral, a universal Vassiliev invariant over
\mathbb{Q}
, in an essential way to reduce the Goussarov–Habiro conjecture to a combinatorial question. Similarly, the existence of a universal Vassiliev invariant over
\mathbb{Z}
would reduce the Goussarov–Habiro conjecture (over
\mathbb{Z}
!) to a purely combinatorial problem. A current candidate for such a universal invariant over
\mathbb{Z}
is the
Goodwillie–Weiss embedding calculus tower

[
Bud+17
,
Kos20
,
BH21
]
. Actually, Conant’s work
[
Con08
]
on the
\STU^{2}
relation was an important ingredient for identifying modules in the second page of the spectral sequence arising in the study of the Goodwillie–Weiss embedding calculus tower
[
Bud+17
,
Shi23
]
. The present work motivates the investigation of the string link analogous of the embedding calculus tower.

At the moment, the best known result about universality of the Goodwillie–Weiss embedding calculus tower is that it is a universal Vassiliev invariant over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
in degree
\leq p+1

[
BH21
, Theorem A]
. Combining a
string link
version of this result with the present work could lead to a proof of the Goussarov–Habiro conjecture over
\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}
in degree
n<p
.

1.4.
Notations

Lower indices usually refer to
degrees
of diagrams and claspers, and upper indices usually refer to filtrations of diagrams by
size
(number of connected components). The notation
\FI
, for
framing independence
, refers to the presence of the relation
\1T
. Sets of diagrams are denoted by
\mathcal{D}
aggremented with various indices, Hopf algebras by
\mathcal{A}
and Lie algebras by
\mathcal{L}
.

Aknowledgements

This work was done under the supervision of Peter Teichner, whom I wish to thank for his guidance. I also wish to thank James Conant for helpful discussions and comments.

2.
Primitive Lie algebra of Feynman diagrams

The goal of this section is to identify the primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
of the Hopf algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
Feynman diagrams on
m
strands.

In the case of knots, i.e.
m=1
, tree diagrams generate the primitive Lie algebra
11
11

11

Since
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is commutative, this Lie algebra has a trivial bracket.

\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}})
of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}}

[
Bar95
]
, which, by
[
Con08
]
, is isomorphic to the space of trees

\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(1)/\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle.

See also
[
Kos20
, Theorem G3]
for a concise presentation of these results. Commutativity of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}}
implies, by the Milnor–Moore theorem, that there is an isomorphism between the primitive part
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}})
and the inseparable quotient
(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}})^{I}
(where any diagram which is a product of non-trivial diagrams is set to zero). This fact is essential in Conant’s result
[
Con08
]
, which actually holds over
\mathbb{Z}
.

In the case of string links, tree diagrams also generate the primitive part
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
. However,
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is not commutative when
m>1
and the argument of Conant does not translate a priori. In this chapter, we prove that the conclusion still holds:
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)/\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle
.

In Section
2.1
, we recall the definitions of the algebra of diagrams
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)

[
Bar95
]
and its bialgebra structure. In particular, we use a slightly different presentation of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)
, by forest diagrams instead of more general Feynman diagrams. This is more convenient for two reasons: the primitive part will be presented as a space of tree diagrams, and tree diagrams are more convenient
12
12

12

It makes the
zip construction
easier to work with.
in Habiro’s clasper calculus
[
Hab00
]
, which is the topic of Chapter
3.1
.

In Section
2.2
, we show that the primitive part of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is indeed generated by tree diagrams. To that end, we introduce the
(restricted) primitive filtration

P^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

[
Car07
]
and compare it with the
size filtration

F^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, defined via the size
13
13

13

The
size
of a forest diagram is its number of connected components, i.e. its number of trees.
of forest diagrams. The main ingredient is the definition of sections
s^{k+1}\colon F^{k+1}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow F^{k}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
to the inclusions
i^{k}\colon F^{k}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\xhookrightarrow{}F^{k+1}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. For a forest diagram
F
of size
k+1
composed of trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{k+1}
, it is given by

s^{k+1}(F)=\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sy_{k+1}}(F-(F)_{\sigma})

where
(F)_{\sigma}\coloneqq T_{\sigma(1)}\cdot\ldots\cdot T_{\sigma(k+1)}
is a
stacked version
of
F
. For
\sigma\in\Sy_{k+1}
, the forest diagram
(F)_{\sigma}
contains the same trees as in
F
, but they are stacked above each other (in the order prescribed by
\sigma
) whereas the trees in
F
may be shuffled together. One can obtain
F
from
(F)_{\sigma}
by a sequence of
slide moves
: exchanges of adjacent legs belonging to distinct trees. By
\STU
, this allows us to write
F-(F)_{\sigma}
as a sum of forest diagrams with
k
trees: for each slide move,
\STU
yields a term where the two involved trees are attached together. In degree
n
, the composition

s^{2}\circ\dots\circ s^{n}\colon\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow P^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}=Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})_{n}

yields a projector onto the submodule of primitive degree
n
diagrams, which is thus generated by tree diagrams.

Section
2.3
develops a combinatorial framework aimed at finding a concrete presentation, by generators and relations, of the primitive part of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, together with a projection from
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
onto it. The idea is simple: to investigate the minimal set of relations required for defining analogous of the inclusions
i^{k}
and sections
s^{k+1}
, but now between concrete
14
14

14

By
concrete
modules, we mean modules presented by explicit generators and relations, not submodules of larger modules.
modules of forest diagrams. In degree
n
, we start with the modules
\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{k}(m)/\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX\rangle
generated by forest diagrams of size
k
and degree
n
, for
1\leq k\leq n
. The
\STU
relation is not
size-homogeneous
and we cannot add it as it is. This is the source of the problem.

First, what are the minimal relations required for defining maps

\tilde{\iota}^{k}\colon\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{k}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX\rangle}\dashrightarrow\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{k+1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX\rangle}\;\text{ ?}

By
\STU
, a size
k
forest should be sent to the difference obtained by breaking apart one trivalent vertex adjacent to a leg. However, the result may depend on the chosen vertex. Modding out by
\STU^{2}
makes it well-defined
[
Con08
]
. We obtain the sequence of maps

(2.1)

\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX\rangle}\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}^{1}_{n}}\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}^{2}_{n}}\dots\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}^{n-1}_{n}}\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}\cong\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

where the rightmost isomorphism follows from the fact that
\STU^{2}
becomes
\4T
for forests of size
n
and degree
n
, i.e. chord diagrams.

Next, what additional relations are required for defining maps from right to left, i.e. sending size
k
forests to sums of smaller forests? Starting from the
ansatz
that the formula

(2.2)

\text{``}\pi^{k}\colon F\mapsto\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sy_{k}}(F-(F)_{\sigma})\text{''}

should work, we need to make sense of the expression “
F-(F)_{\sigma}
”. More generally, we need to make sense of
F-F^{\prime}
for any two forests
F,F^{\prime}
made up of the same collection of trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
, but shuffled in different ways. As when working inside
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
where
\STU
holds, the expression
F-F^{\prime}
should equal a sum of smaller forest diagrams obtained by going from
F^{\prime}
to
F
through a sequence of slide moves. Such a sequence of slide moves is interpreted as a
path
in the
graph of forests

\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
, whose vertices are the forests made up of the trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
and whose (undirected) edges are given by
slide moves
: exchanges of adjacent legs belonging to distinct trees. Such an edge is represented by “
\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=15.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\leftrightarrow\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=15.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}
”, where it is understood that both sides represent forest diagrams that are identical outside of the part shown, which involves legs of two distinct trees. To an oriented edge
F_{\times}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=15.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\rightarrow F_{=}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=15.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}
one can associate the forest diagram

\overrightarrow{F_{\times}F_{=}}=\overrightarrow{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\right)\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}\right)}\coloneqq\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}},

in such a way that
F_{=}-F_{\times}=\overrightarrow{F_{\times}F_{=}}
is an
\STU
relation, when considering all the terms as elements of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Then, to a path
P
from
F
to
F^{\prime}
one can associate an element

\overrightarrow{P}\in\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle},

equal to the sum of forests associated to the edges traversed by
P
. In order to be able to write “
F-F^{\prime}=\overrightarrow{P}
”, we need to ensure that the expression
\overrightarrow{P}
does not depend on the chosen path. Two different paths
P,P^{\prime}
from
F
to
F^{\prime}
differ by a cycle
C
, and
\overrightarrow{P^{\prime}}=\overrightarrow{P}+\overrightarrow{C}
. Therefore, we need to mod out the target module of forests by all
\overrightarrow{C}
, where
C
runs over all cycles in graphs of forests of size
k
. However, this generates a large and non-explicit set of relations.

In order to obtain a smaller set of relations, we need to consider
graphs of labelled forests
15
15

15

The graph of labelled forests
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
differs from the graph of forests
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
only when some trees among
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
are identical, in which case a given forest has multiple possible labelings.
, denoted
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
, whose vertices are forest diagrams made of
T_{1},\dots,T_{k}
, with the difference that we remember the labeling of their trees by
1,\dots,k
. The edges are also given by slide moves. If the tree diagram
T_{i}
has
n^{j}_{i}
legs on the
j
-th strand, for
1\leq i\leq k
and
1\leq j\leq m
, then edges of
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
correspond to basic transpositions in the product of permutation groups
\Sy_{n^{1}}\times\dots\times\Sy_{n^{m}}
where
n^{j}\coloneqq\sum_{i}n^{j}_{i}
. More precisely, the edge (slide move) permuting the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-th legs (belonging to distinct trees) on the
j
-th strand corresponds to the transposition
\tau_{i}^{j}=(i,i+1)
in the factor
\Sy_{n^{j}}
. Thus, a cycle
C
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
corresponds to a permutation
\tau_{k_{l-1}}\dots\tau_{k_{0}}
written as a product of basic transpositions. Since
C
is a cycle, the product
\tau_{k_{l-1}}\dots\tau_{k_{0}}
must equal the identity. Using the presentation

(2.3)

\Sy_{n^{1}}\times\dots\times\Sy_{n^{m}}=\left\langle\begin{matrix}\tau^{(1)}_{1}\dots\tau^{(1)}_{n^{1}-1}\\
\cdots\\
\tau^{(m)}_{1}\dots\tau^{(m)}_{n^{m}-1}\end{matrix}\left|\begin{matrix}\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i}=1&\text{for each }i,j\\
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}=\tau^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}\tau^{(j)}_{i}&\text{if }\lvert i-i^{\prime}\rvert>1\\
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i+1}\tau^{(j)}_{i}=\tau^{(j)}_{i+1}\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i+1}&\text{for each }i,j\\
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}=\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}&\text{if }j\neq j^{\prime}\end{matrix}\right.\right\rangle

of the product of groups of permutations
\Sy_{n^{1}}\times\dots\times\Sy_{n^{m}}
, we can turn
\tau_{k_{l-1}}\dots\tau_{k_{0}}
into the empty word by a sequence of relations in (
2.3
). Translating this sequence of relations turns the cycle
C
into a composition of
back-and-forth paths
(
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i}=1
), squares for each
far-commutativity relation
(
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}=\tau^{(j)}_{i^{\prime}}\tau^{(j)}_{i}
when
\lvert i-i^{\prime}\rvert>1
and
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}=\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}
when
j\neq j^{\prime}
) and hexagons for each
braiding relation
(
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j)}_{i+1}\tau^{(j)}_{i}=\tau^{(j)}_{i+1}\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau_{i+1}
). Therefore, the element
\overrightarrow{C}\in\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(m)
is a sum of
\AS
(Figure
3
),
\STU^{2}
(
1.4
) and
\hexagon
relations (
1.6
).

The above paragraph concerns cycles in graphs of labelled forests
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
, while we actually need to consider cycles in graphs of forests
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
. However, there is a surjection
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
and any path
P
below lifts to a path
\tilde{P}
above, after a choice of labelling
\tilde{F_{0}}
of the starting point
F_{0}
of
P
. Moreover,
\overrightarrow{\tilde{P}}=\overrightarrow{P}
. If
P
is a cycle, i.e.
F_{l}=F_{0}
, then
\tilde{P}
is not necessarily a cycle: even though its endpoints
\tilde{F_{l}}
and
\tilde{F_{0}}
have the same underlying
unlabelled
forest
F_{l}=F_{0}
, their labeling may differ by a permutation
\sigma
. Lifting
P
again, but now starting from
\tilde{F_{l}}
, and repeating this
N
times, where
N
equals the order of
\sigma
in
\Sy_{n}
, yields a cycle in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
which lifts the
N
-fold composition of the cycle
P
in
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{k})
. Thus
N\cdot\overrightarrow{C}
is a sum of
\AS,\STU^{2}
and
\hexagon
relations as well, hence so is
\overrightarrow{C}
, since we are working over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Therefore, for two size
k
forests
F
and
F^{\prime}
made up of the same trees, we can finally define

\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}F}\coloneqq\overrightarrow{P}\in\mathcal{F}^{\FI,k-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k-1}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle},

and in Section
2.4.3
we can make sense of the
ansatz
(
2.2
) by defining

(2.4)

\tilde{\pi}^{k}\colon\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\FI,k-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon F\mapsto\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sy_{k}}\overrightarrow{(F)_{\sigma}F}.

After checking that the maps
\tilde{\iota}
’s and
\tilde{\pi}
’s satisfy all the relations, the sequence of maps (
2.1
) becomes a sequence of inclusions, with the
\pi
’s as sections:

(2.5)

In Section
2.4
, we gather the above results and identify the filtration (
2.5
) with the primitive or size filtration
F^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The leftmost module in (
2.5
) is denoted
\mathcal{L}^{\FI}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, it is the degree
n
part of the
Lie algebra of trees
. The rightmost module is
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, it is the degree
n
part of the
Hopf algebra of forests
16
16

16

It is the well-known Hopf algebra of chord diagrams or, equivalenlty, Feynman diagrams.
. All in all, this shows that
\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{L}^{\FI}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
injects into
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
and is isomorphic to the primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since the
\hexagon
relation requires at least two trees, it is trivial in
\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We finally obtain, as desired,

\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})\cong\frac{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}.

2.1.
Algebras of diagrams

This section recalls the definition of chord and Feynman diagrams
[
Bar95
]
on tangle skeletons, as well as their bialgebra structure.

2.1.1.
Uni-trivalent graphs.

A
uni-trivalent graph
, also called
Feynman
or
Jacobi graph
, is an undirected graph
G=(V=U\sqcup T,E)
consisting of univalent and trivalent vertices (forming the sets
U
and
T
, respectively). The edges are defined as pairs of distinct vertices
17
17

17

We could allow diagrams with edges attached to a single vertex, i.e.
tadpoles
, but those will vanish by
\AS
.
. The trivalent vertices are endowed with a cyclic orientation of their three adjacent edges. When a uni-trivalent graph is drawn, the cyclic orientations are the counterclockwise ones unless indicated otherwise.

The univalent and trivalent vertices are often called
leaves
and
nodes
, respectively. An edge adjacent to a leaf is called a
leg
.

The
degree
of a uni-trivalent graph
G
is
\deg(G)\coloneqq\frac{\lvert V\rvert}{2}
.

A uni-trivalent graph
G
is a
forest
if it has no cycles and a
tree
if in addition it has a single component. Note that, if
G
is a tree, then
\deg(G)=\lvert U\rvert-1=\lvert T\rvert+1
.

2.1.2.
Uni-trivalent forest and chord diagrams.

Let
S
be an oriented
1
-manifold with boundary. Later
S
will be the underlying manifold of what will be referred to as a
tangle skeleton
. Since this work focuses on string links,
S
will usually be a disjoint union of intervals
\{1,\dots,m\}\times[0,1]
.

A
uni-trivalent
or
Feynman
or
Jacobi diagram
on
S
is a pair
D=(G,[u])
where
G=(U\sqcup T,E)
is a uni-trivalent graph and
[u]
is the isotopy class of an embedding
u\colon U\xhookrightarrow{}S-\del S
of the univalent vertices into the interior of
S
. The set of uni-trivalent diagrams on
S
is denoted
\mathcal{D}(S)
.

The
degree
of a uni-trivalent diagram is the degree of its underlying graph. Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{n}(S)\subset\mathcal{D}(S)
the subset of degree
n
uni-trivalent diagrams. This endows
\mathcal{D}(S)
with a graded set structure.

A uni-trivalent diagram is a
forest
, resp.
tree
, diagram if its underlying graph is a forest, resp. a tree. A forest diagram is a
chord diagram
if it has no nodes. Denote by
\mathcal{D}^{F}(S),\mathcal{D}^{T}(S),\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)
the corresponding graded subsets of
\mathcal{D}(S)
. The
size
of a forest diagram is its number of trees, i.e. the number
b_{0}(G)
of connected components of the underlying uni-trivalent graph
G
. For example, the forest diagram on Figure
5(b)
has size
3
. For
s\geq 0
, the set of forest diagrams of size
s
is denoted
\mathcal{D}^{s}(S)
. Note that
\mathcal{D}^{n}_{n}(S)=\mathcal{D}^{c}_{n}(S)
and
\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)\coloneqq\mathcal{D}^{1}(m)
is the set of tree diagrams.

(a)

Chord diagram

(b)

Forest diagram

(c)

Feynman diagram

Figure 5
.
Examples of diagrams

By
[
Bar95
]
, the inclusions
\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)\subset\mathcal{D}^{F}(S)\subset\mathcal{D}(S)
induce isomorphisms
18
18

18

In
[
Bar95
]
, it is shown that
\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{D}(S)
induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)/\langle\4T\rangle\cong\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}(S)/\langle\STU\rangle
. Thus, we get
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)/\langle\4T\rangle\xhookrightarrow{}\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{F}(S)/\langle\STU\rangle\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}(S)/\langle\STU\rangle
and the first map is surjective since any forest can be written as a sum of chord diagrams by applying
\STU
until it has no node left. Hence the first map is an isomorphism, so is the second since their composition is an isomorphism. The same holds with
\FI
added everywhere.
of graded
\mathbb{Z}
-modules

(2.6)

\mathcal{A}(S)\coloneqq\frac{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{c}(S)}{\langle\4T\rangle}\cong\frac{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{F}(S)}{\langle\STU\rangle}\cong\frac{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}(S)}{\langle\STU\rangle}

and the relations
\AS,\IHX
are consequences of
\STU
(see Figures
2
and
3
for descriptions of all the involved relations). Unless stated otherwise, we use the presentation of
\mathcal{A}(S)
using forest diagram. The degree
n
part of
\mathcal{A}(S)
is denoted
\mathcal{A}_{n}(S)
. The only degree
0
diagram is the empty one, hence
\mathcal{A}_{0}(S)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

2.1.3.
Framed versus unframed.

There is an
unframed
or
framing independent
(
\FI
) version of the module of Feynman diagrams

(2.7)

\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(S)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(S)/\langle\1T\rangle,

where
\1T
is the submodule generated by all diagrams containing an
isolated chord
, i.e. a chord whose endpoints lie on the same strand with no other endpoint in between them.

Writing
A^{(FI)}(S)
indicates that the statement holds for both
A(S)
and
A^{FI}(S)
.

2.1.4.
Coalgebra structure.

The graded
\mathbb{Z}
-module
\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(S)
has a structure of coalgebra, with comultiplication
[
Bar95
, Definition 3.7]

(2.8)

\Delta(D)\coloneqq\sum_{J\subset\pi_{0}(D)}D_{J}\otimes D_{\pi_{0}(D)-J},

where the sum runs over all the subsets
J
of the set
\pi_{0}(D)\coloneqq\pi_{0}(G)
of connected components of
D
and
D_{J}
denotes the subdiagram of
D
consisting of those components contained in
J
. The counit
\epsilon
is given by
\epsilon(D)=1
if
D
is the empty diagram, and it is zero otherwise.

For example,

\displaystyle\Delta\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/123.eps}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/empty.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/123.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/23.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/13.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/12.eps}}}

\displaystyle\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/12.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/13.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/23.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/123.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/empty.eps}}}.

Note that the comultiplication is co-associative and co-commutative.

2.1.5.
Hopf algebra of diagrams on string links.

When
S=\{1,\dots,m\}\times[0,1]
is a disjoint union of intervals, we write
\mathcal{D}(m)
for
\mathcal{D}(S)
,
\mathcal{A}(m)
for
\mathcal{A}(S)
, and so on. In that case, vertical stacking of diagrams additionaly endows
\mathcal{A}(m)
and
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)
with an algebra structure. More precisely,
D\cdot D^{\prime}
denotes the vertical concatenation of
D
and
D^{\prime}
, with
D
above
D^{\prime}
. The unit
1
is the empty diagram.

Proposition 2.1.5.1

(Bar-Natan
[
Bar95
, Proposition 3.9]
)
.

The comultiplication and multiplication defined above endow
\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)
with the structure of a connected co-commutative Hopf algebra
19
19

19

Bar-Natan does not explicit the existence of an antipodal over
\mathbb{Z}
, but one can check that we can define it similarly to the antipodal in the
Connes-Kreimer
Hopf algebra.
. It is commutative when
m=1
.

2.2.
Primitive filtration of forest diagrams

2.2.1.
Primitive filtration of a Hopf algebra.

Let us recall some basic notions about primitive elements in Hopf algebras. See
[
MM65
]
,
[
Mon93
, Chapter 5]
,
[
Car07
]
and
[
Ron11
]
for more details.

Fix a Hopf algebra
A
over
\mathbb{Q}
with multiplication
\mu
or
\cdot
, unit
1
, comultiplication
\Delta
and counit
\epsilon
. An element
x\in A
is
primitive
if
\Delta(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
. A direct calculation shows that the commutator of two primitive elements is primitive as well. In particular, the set
\Prim(A)
of primitive elements in
A
is a Lie algebra, called the
primitive Lie algebra of
A
. Conversely, to any Lie algebra
L
one can associate its
universal enveloping algebra

U(L)
, which is a Hopf algebra. The Milnor–Moore theorem states conditions under which the functors
\Prim
and
U
are inverses of each other.

Theorem 2.2.1.1

(Milnor–Moore theorem
[
Mon93
, Theorem 5.6.5]
)
.

Let
A
be a connected
20
20

20

A Hopf algebra
A
is
connected
if
A_{0}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}\cdot 1
is its unique simple subcoalgebra.
and co-commutative Hopf algebra over a characteristic zero field, then the inclusion
\Prim(A)\xhookrightarrow{}A
induces an isomorphism of Hopf algebras
U(\Prim(A))\cong A
.

Now, let us assume that
A
is a connected and co-commutative Hopf algebra over
\mathbb{Q}
, so that
U(\Prim(A))\cong A
. The
primitive filtration
of
A
is the filtration

(2.9)

\mathbb{Q}\cdot 1=P^{0}A\xhookrightarrow{}P^{1}A\xhookrightarrow{}P^{2}A\xhookrightarrow{}\dots

where
P^{k}A
is the
\mathbb{Q}
-submodule of
A
additively generated by
\mathbb{Q}\cdot 1
and all products of
\leq k
-many primitive elements. In particular
P^{1}A=\mathbb{Q}\oplus\Prim(A)
and any element
x\in A
is in some
P^{k}A
, for some
k
, since
A
is primitively generated. Under the isomorphism
U(\Prim(A))\cong A
, the primitive filtration coincides with the
Lie filtration
of
U(\Prim(A))

[
MM65
, Prop. 5.17]
.

For all
k\geq 0
, we have
P^{k}A=\mathbb{Q}\oplus P^{k}A_{\geq 1}
where
A_{\geq 1}\coloneqq A_{+}
is the kernel of the counit. The filtration

(2.10)

0=P^{0}A_{\geq 1}\xhookrightarrow{}P^{1}A_{\geq 1}\xhookrightarrow{}P^{2}A_{\geq 1}\xhookrightarrow{}\dots

is called the
reduced primitive filtration
of
A_{\geq 1}
.

2.2.2.
Primitive filtration of forests.

The focus returns to the Hopf algebra of forest diagrams on
m
strands,
\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, which is indeed connected and co-commutative, hence the Milnor–Moore theorem applies, i.e.
U(\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}))\cong\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, and it is filtered by the primitive filtration.

In the case
m=1
, Bar-Natan showed that
\Prim(\mathcal{A}(1)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is (additively) generated by all connected diagrams
[
Bar95
]
and his argument translates well for
m\geq 1
. Actually, this statement is also true over
\mathbb{Z}
, as shown by Lando in
[
Lan94
]
via a beautiful counting argument. Unfortunately, the argument uses commutativity in
\mathcal{A}(1)
, hence it does not translate directly to the general case
m\geq 1
. Instead, we give an argument that compares the primitive filtration to the size filtration defined below, in a way parallel to the ideas of the subsequent subsections in this section. First, let us introduce some notations.

Definition 2.2.2.1
.

The
size filtration
of
\mathcal{A}(m)
is the filtration
\{F^{k}\mathcal{A}(m)\}_{k\geq 0}
where
F^{k}\mathcal{A}(m)
is the
\mathbb{Z}
-submodule generated by forest diagrams of size
\leq k
. In degree
n\geq 0
, it induces a filtration
21
21

21

The only forest diagram of size
0
is the empty diagram
1
, hence
F^{0}\mathcal{A}(m)=\mathbb{Z}\cdot 1
.

(2.11)

F^{0}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)\xhookrightarrow{}F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)\xhookrightarrow{}\dots\xhookrightarrow{}F^{n-1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)\xhookrightarrow{}F^{n}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)=\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)

of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)
. The
size filtration
of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)
is defined in the same way, with
\FI
added everywhere. Tensoring everything with
\mathbb{Q}
yields the size filtration of
\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For
F\in\mathcal{D}^{F}(m)
a forest diagram, define the
reduced co-multiplication

\overline{\Delta}(F)
as

(2.12)

\overline{\Delta}(F)\coloneqq\sum_{\emptyset\neq J\subsetneq\pi_{0}(F)}\left(F_{J}\right)\otimes\left(F_{\pi_{0}(F)-J}\right)

where
F_{J}
denotes the subdiagram of
F
that consists of the components contained in
J
. In other words,
\overline{\Delta}(F)=\Delta(F)-F\otimes 1-1\otimes F
. Thus,
\overline{\Delta}
satisfies
\AS,\IHX,\STU
(and
\1T
) and descends to a well-defined map

(2.13)

\overline{\Delta}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m).

Define recursively
\overline{\Delta}^{k}\coloneqq(\overline{\Delta}^{k-1}\otimes\id)\circ\overline{\Delta}\colon\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)\longrightarrow(\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m))^{\otimes k+1}
, for
k>1
. Concretely, we have

(2.14)

\overline{\Delta}^{k}(F)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J_{0}\sqcup\dots\sqcup J_{k}=\pi_{0}(F)\\
J_{i}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}\left(F_{J_{0}}\right)\otimes\dots\otimes\left(F_{J_{k}}\right),

where the sum is over all possible partitions of the components of
F
into
k+1
nonempty subforests.

For example, we have

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/123.eps}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/23.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/13.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/12.eps}}}

\displaystyle\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/12.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/13.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/23.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}

and

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}^{2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/123.eps}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}

\displaystyle+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\;+\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/3.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/1.eps}}}\otimes\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/Delta/2.eps}}}.

Note also that the reduced primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
can already be defined over
\mathbb{Z}
: for
k\geq 0
,
P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)
is generated by all products of
\leq k
-many primitive elements.

Lemma 2.2.2.2
.

We have the following properties:

(i)

For all
x,y\in\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)
,

(2.15)

\overline{\Delta}(x\cdot y)=\overline{\Delta}(x)\cdot\overline{\Delta}(y)+\overline{\Delta}(x)\cdot(1\otimes y+y\otimes 1)+(1\otimes x+x\otimes 1)\cdot\overline{\Delta}(y)+x\otimes y+y\otimes x;

(ii)

\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m))=\ker(\overline{\Delta})
;

(iii)

\overline{\Delta}(P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m))\subset\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P^{i}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)\otimes P^{k-i}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)
;

(iv)

P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{\geq_{1}}(m)\subset\ker(\overline{\Delta}^{k})
;

(v)

F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{\geq_{1}}(m)\subset\ker(\overline{\Delta}^{k})
, i.e.
\overline{\Delta}^{k}
vanishes on forests of size
\leq k
.

All of the above properties remain true after passing to coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
.

Proof.

Part (i) follows from the definition of
\overline{\Delta}
and compatibility
\Delta(x\cdot y)=\Delta(x)\cdot\Delta(y)
. Part (ii) is immediate. Part (v) follows from the description (
2.14
), since for a forest
F
of size
k
the sum is empty. It remains to show (iii) and (iv).

We prove (iii) by induction on
k
. To lighten notation, we write
P^{i}
for
P^{i}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)
. The base case,
k=1
, is settled by (ii). Let
1\leq k<n
and assume that
\overline{\Delta}(P^{k})\subset\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P^{i}\otimes P^{k-i}
. The filtration stage
P^{k+1}
is generated by
P^{k}
together with all products of exactly
k+1
primitive elements. Consider an element
z\in P^{k+1}
of the latter sort, i.e.
z=xy
for
x\in P^{k}
and
y\in P^{1}=\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m))
. Using
\overline{\Delta}(y)=0
(by (ii)), (
2.15
) becomes

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}(z)=\overline{\Delta}(x)\cdot(1\otimes y+y\otimes 1)+x\otimes y+y\otimes x.

Now, by induction hypothesis, we have
\overline{\Delta}(x)\in\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P^{i}\otimes P^{k-i}
. Using
P^{i}\cdot y\subset P^{i+1}
, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}(z)\in\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P^{i}\otimes P^{k+1-i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P^{i+1}\otimes P^{k-i}+P^{k}\otimes P^{1}+P^{1}\otimes P^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}P^{i}\otimes P^{k+1-i},

which proves (iv).

The proof of (iv) also proceeds by induction on
k
, with the base case being (ii) again. Assume that
P^{k-1}\subset\ker(\overline{\Delta}^{k-1})
. Let
z\in P^{k}
. By (iv) we can write

\overline{\Delta}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j}x_{ij}\otimes y_{ij}

where
x_{ij}\in P^{i}
and
y_{ij}\in P^{k-i}
. Then

\overline{\Delta}^{k}(z)\coloneqq(\overline{\Delta}^{k-1}\otimes\id)\circ\overline{\Delta}(z)=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\sum_{j}\overline{\Delta}^{k-1}(x_{ij})\otimes y_{ij}=0

since all
\overline{\Delta}^{k-1}(x_{ij})=0
by induction hypothesis. This concludes the proof of (iv).
∎

Theorem 2.2.2.3
.

Over
\mathbb{Q}
, the reduced primitive filtration and the size filtration of
\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
coincide. In particular,
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is the submodule of
\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
generated by tree diagrams.

When
m=1
, this already holds over
\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

We omit
\FI
in the notation since the proof for that case is the same.

In degree
n=0
, there is nothing to prove. Fix a degree
n>0
. First, any forest diagram of size
1
is primitive by definition of the co-multiplication, i.e.
F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset P^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

In fact,
F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset P^{1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
implies that
F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\subset P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
for each
k\geq 1
. We show this by induction
22
22

22

This argument is similar to the proof of Proposition 6.10 in
[
Hab00
]
, which deals with forest claspers instead of forest diagrams.
. The base case is the above paragraph. Assume that
F^{k}\subset P^{k}
and consider a a forest diagram
F
of size
k+1
. Pick any tree
T
in
F
and apply a sequence
F=F_{0}\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow F_{r}
of
\STU
-relations to slide the legs of
T
above the rest of the trees. Thus,
F_{r}=T\cdot(F\setminus T)\subset P^{1}\cdot F^{k}\subset P^{1}\cdot P^{k}\subset P^{k+1}
. For each
i
,
F_{i}-F_{i+1}
is a forest of size
k
by
\STU
, i.e.
F_{i}-F_{i+1}\in F^{k}\subset P^{k}
. Therefore,

F=\sum_{i=0}^{r-1}(F_{i}-F_{i+1})+F_{r}\subset P^{k+1}.

Consequently, the size filtration injects into the primitive filtration:

We show that the vertical arrows are all equalities, inductively from right to left. Let
1\leq k<n
and assume that
F^{k+1}=P^{k+1}
. We seek for a section to the inclusion
i^{k}_{n}
as in the diagram below

(2.16)
