Consider the linear map

(2.17)

\begin{split}s^{k+1}_{n}\colon F^{k+1}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}&\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\\
F&\longmapsto F-\tfrac{1}{(k+1)!}\mu^{k}\circ\overline{\Delta}^{k}(F),\end{split}

where
\mu^{k}\colon\mathcal{A}(m)^{\otimes k+1}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}(m)
is the
m
-fold multiplication. We have:

•

The image of
s^{k+1}_{n}
lies in
F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Indeed, for a forest diagram
F
of size
k+1
, say
F=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k+1}T_{i}
, we have
23
23

23

There is no natural ordering of the trees of
F
, hence a choice of identification
\pi_{0}(F)\cong\{1,\dots,s\}
is required. However, the sum in (
2.18
) is over all permutations, hence this choice is irrelevant.

(2.18)

s^{k+1}_{n}(F)=\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sy_{k+1}}(F-(F)_{\sigma})

where
(F)_{\sigma}\coloneqq T_{\sigma(1)}\dots T_{\sigma(s)}
is obtained by stacking the trees of
F
in the order prescribed by
\sigma
. Each difference
(F-(F)_{\sigma})
can be obtained by a sequence of leg exchanges (a path in
\mathcal{F}(F)
, see Definition
2.3.3.1
) from
F_{\sigma}
to
F
, hence we obtain
F-F_{\sigma}
as a sum of forests of size
k
(each leg exchange yields a term where the two legs are attached together, by
\STU
). Thus
s^{k+1}_{n}(F)\in F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

For example,

\displaystyle s^{3}_{3}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-term-F}}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-term-F}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-term-F12.eps}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-term-F}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-term-F21.eps}}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-difference-term-F12.eps}}}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-diff-term-F21-1.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/section_primitive_filtration/section_primitive_filtration-diff-term-F21-2.eps}}}\right).

•

The map
s^{k+1}_{n}
is a section to the inclusion
i^{k}_{n}
.
Indeed, for
F\in F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have
\overline{\Delta}^{k}(F)=0
by Lemma
2.2.2.2
(vi). Thus
s^{k+1}_{n}
restricts to the identity on
F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

By Lemma
2.2.2.2
(v),
s^{k+1}_{n}
also restricts to the identity on
P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Thus, starting with
x\in P^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
and going around the diagram (
2.16
) in the counterclockwise direction, we end up with
s(x)=x
, which shows that the vertical inclusion
F^{k}\xhookrightarrow{}P^{k}
is also surjective. This concludes the proof that the two filtrations coincide.

It follows that

\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})=P^{1}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}=F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}},

i.e. the primitive part of
\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is additively generated by forests of size
1
, that is, trees.

When
m=1
, the bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(m)
is commutative. Thus, if
F
is a forest of size
k+1
, then all
(F)_{\sigma}
,
\sigma\in\Sy_{k+1}
, are equal and averaging is not needed. In particular, the section
s^{k+1}_{n}
defined in (
2.17
) is already defined over
\mathbb{Z}
. This yields an alternative proof of the fact that tree diagrams generate the primitive Lie algebra of
\mathcal{A}(1)
over
\mathbb{Z}
. See
[
Lan94
]
for a different argument.
∎

In order to better understand that filtration, and more particularly the primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
, a concrete description of the filtration steps, i.e. by generators and relations, is required. This is the topic of the next two sections.

2.3.
Permutographs and graphs of forests

2.3.1.
Permutographs.

A
n
-permutohedron
is a polytope defined as the convex hull of a set of the form

(2.19)

\{(a_{\sigma(1)},\dots,a_{\sigma(n)})\mid\sigma\in\Sy_{n}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

where
\textbf{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{R}
. When
\textbf{a}=(1,2,\dots,n)
, we call (
2.19
) the
standard
n
-permutohedron
. They are rich combinatorial objects that appear naturally in many places
[
Zie12
,
Pos09
,
LTV10
]
. For example, their faces classify the labelled partitions of finite sets.

To study graphs of forests, we will actually focus on
2
-skeleta of permutohedra. For a tuple of non-negative integers
\textbf{n}\coloneqq(n_{1},\dots,n_{s})\in\mathbb{N}^{s}
, consider the set
V_{\textbf{n}}
of all possible words made of
n_{i}
-many
i
, for each
i=1,\dots,s
. In particular, we write
\underline{w}_{\textbf{n}}\coloneqq\underbrace{1\dots 1}_{n_{1}}\dots\underbrace{s\dots s}_{n_{s}}
and
\overline{w}_{\textbf{n}}\coloneqq\underbrace{s\dots s}_{n_{s}}\dots\underbrace{1\dots 1}_{n_{1}}
. The symmetric group
\Sy_{n}
, where
n\coloneqq\sum_{i}n_{i}
, acts on
V_{\textbf{n}}
by permutation, denoted
\sigma\cdot w
for
\sigma\in\Sy_{n},w\in V_{\textbf{n}}
. The action is transitive but has many fixed points, unless all
n_{i}=1
.

Definition 2.3.1.1
.

The
n
-permutohedron graph
, or
n
-permutograph
, is the undirected graph
P_{\textbf{n}}\coloneqq(V_{\textbf{n}},E_{\textbf{n}})
, whose set of vertices is
V_{\textbf{n}}
and two distinct vertices
v,w\in V_{\textbf{n}}
are connected by an undirected edge if there is an basic transposition
\tau_{i}=(i,i+1)\in\Sy_{n}
such that
w=\tau\cdot v
(hence also
v=\tau\cdot w
), i.e.
v=\dots jk\dots
and
w=\dots kj\dots
where
1\leq j\neq k\leq s
are the
i
-th and
(i+1)
-st entries of
v,w
.

There is at most one edge joining any pair of vertices and at most
n-1
edges adjacent to a given vertex, since there are only
n-1
adjacent transpositions
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n-1}
. Note that permutographs are connected, since the
\Sy_{\textbf{n}}
-action on
V_{\textbf{n}}
is transitive.

(a)

The permutograph
P_{(2,1,1)}

(b)

Graph of labelled legs on one strands

Figure 6
.
One slice of a graph of labelled forests

Proposition 2.3.1.2
.

The first homology group of the permutograph
P_{\textbf{n}}
is generated by length
4
and
6
cycles. In other words, any
1
-cycle in
P_{\textbf{n}}
is the boundary of a union of squares and hexagons.

Proof.

To prove this, we scan
P_{\textbf{n}}
using the
height function

(2.20)

h\colon V_{\textbf{n}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}\\
\colon v\longmapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\#\{j>i\mid v_{j}<v_{i}\}

which assigns to a word
v
the number
h(v)
of pairs of letters
\dots v_{i}\dots v_{j}\dots
where
v_{j}<v_{i}
appearing in it, i.e. the number of inversions in
v
. For example,
h(\underline{w}_{\textbf{n}})=0
is minimal and
h(\overline{w}_{\textbf{n}})
is maximal in
P_{\textbf{n}}
. The function
h
is a Morse function in the sense that, for any edge
\{v,w\}
, we have
h(w)=h(v)\pm 1
. Pictorially, when drawing
P_{\textbf{n}}
with all vertices of height
i
at height
i
, no edge is horizontal (see Figure
6(a)
).

Now we show that any
1
-cycle
C
in
P_{\textbf{n}}
is a sum of length
4
and
6
cycles, by double induction on
\max_{v\in C}h(v)
and on the number of vertices reaching this maximum. Up to decomposing
C
into smaller subcycles and removing paths going back and forth, we can assume that
C=\overrightarrow{v^{0}v^{1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{v^{l-1}v^{l}}
where
(v^{0},\dots,v^{l}=v_{0})
is a sequence without repetitions (except (
v^{l}=v^{0}
). If
\max_{v\in C}h(v)=0
then there is nothing to prove since
C=0
.

Assume the statement to be true for all cycles of maximal height
<\max_{v\in C}h(v)
and with less maxima than
C
. Pick any vertex
v^{M}\in C
that reaches the maximum, then we must have
h(v^{M\pm 1})=h(v^{M})-1
. By definition of the permutograph,
v^{M-1}=\tau_{i}\cdot v^{M}
and
v^{M+1}=\tau_{j}\cdot v^{M}
, where
i\neq j
. We assume
i<j
to simplify notation. There are two cases to consider:

(1)

If
\lvert j-i\rvert>1
, then
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
have disjoint support and the vertex
\tilde{v}^{M}\coloneqq\tau_{i}\tau_{j}\cdot v^{M}=\tau_{i}\cdot v^{M+1}=\tau_{j}\cdot v^{M-1}
forms a square together with
v^{M},v^{M+1},v^{M-1}
(we use the relation
\tau_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}
in
\Sy_{n}
). In particular, we have

(2.21)

\begin{split}C&=\overrightarrow{v^{0}v^{1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{v^{M-1}v^{M}}+\overrightarrow{v^{M}v^{M+1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{v^{l-1}v^{l}}\\
&=\underbrace{(\overrightarrow{v^{0}v^{1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{v^{M-1}\tilde{v}^{M}}+\overrightarrow{\tilde{v}^{M}v^{M+1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{v^{l-1}v^{l}})}_{\eqqcolon C^{\prime}}+c=C^{\prime}+c\end{split}

where
c\coloneqq\overrightarrow{v^{M-1}v^{M}}+\overrightarrow{v^{M}v^{M+1}}-\overrightarrow{v^{M-1}\tilde{v}^{M}}-\overrightarrow{\tilde{v}^{M}v^{M+1}}
is a length
4
cycle, and
C^{\prime}
has one less maximum. We are done by induction.

(2)

If
\lvert j-i\rvert=1
, say
j=i+1
, then the above does not work since the vertices
\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1}\cdot v^{M}
and
\tau_{i+1}\tau_{i}\cdot v^{M}
are distinct. In this case we need to dive lower in the permutograph and use instead the relation
\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1}\tau_{i}=\tau_{i+1}\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1}
in
\Sy_{n}
. Note that
v_{i}^{M}>v_{i+1}^{M}>v_{i+2}^{M}
since both
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{i+1}
need to reduce the number of inversions. We find an hexagon in
P_{\textbf{n}}
as the one at the bottom of Figure
6(a)
, where
(v^{M}_{i},v^{M}_{i+1},v^{M}_{i+2})=(3,2,1)
. In particular,
C=C^{\prime}+c
with
C^{\prime}\coloneqq C-c
where
c
is the hexagon cycle. Again,
C^{\prime}
has less maxima and we are done by induction.

∎

Remark 2.3.1.3
.

Recall that the symmetric group
\Sy_{n}
has the following presentation

(2.22)

\Sy_{n}=\left\langle\left.\tau_{1}\dots\tau_{n-1}\right|\begin{matrix}\tau_{i}\tau_{i}=1&\text{for each }i\\
\tau_{i}\tau_{j}=\tau_{j}\tau_{i}&\text{if }\lvert i-j\rvert>1\\
\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1}\tau_{i}=\tau_{i+1}\tau_{i}\tau_{i+1}&\text{for each }i\end{matrix}\right\rangle

where
\tau_{i}=(i,i+1)
,
i=1,\dots,n-1
, are the standard transpositions. The second and third relations are called
far-commutativity
and
braiding
relations.

A cycle in the permutograph
P_{\textbf{n}}
can be written as a presentation of the identity permutation
\id=\tau_{k_{l-1}}\dots\tau_{k_{0}}
as a product of transpositions
\tau_{i}
. In the proof, the assumption that the cycle has no repetitions implies that
k_{i}\neq k_{i+1}
for each
i
, which we can assume since each
\tau_{i}
has order two and cancelling paths going back and forth is already taken into account in the first homology group.

The rest of the proof follows from the realization that, up to removing
\tau_{i}^{2}
when necessary, we can make the word
\tau_{k_{l-1}}\dots\tau_{k_{0}}
trivial by exclusively using far-commutativity relations and braiding relations. The former are responsible for length
4
cycles, while the latter are responsible for length
6
cycles.

Since the action of
\Sy_{n}
on the vertices of
P_{\textbf{n}}
is not free, more details are needed to turn this remark into a rigorous proof.
∎

Definition 2.3.1.4
.

For two graphs
G_{1},G_{2}
, their
cartesian
or
box product
, denoted
G_{1}\Box G_{2}
, is the
1
-skeleton of
G_{1}\times G_{2}
. The box product of graphs is associative.

Corollary 2.3.1.5
.

The first homology of a box product of permutographs
\Box_{j=1}^{m}P_{\textbf{n}^{j}}
is generated by length
4
and
6
cycles.

Proof.

The exact same proof as Proposition
2.3.1.2
works, where now the two transpositions in case 1 can happen in different factors. Consequently, there are two types of length
4
cycles: product of edges from different factors and squares contained in one factor.
∎

Remark 2.3.1.6
.

Following Remark
2.3.1.3
, the above Proposition follows from the fact that the product
\Sy_{n^{1}}\times\dots\times\Sy_{n^{m}}
is generated by standard transpositions
\tau^{(j)}_{1},\dots,\tau^{(j)}_{n^{j}}
, for
j=1,\dots,m
, with the same relations as (
2.22
) for each fixed
j
, with additional commutativity relations
\tau^{(j)}_{i}\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}=\tau^{(j^{\prime})}_{i^{\prime}}\tau^{(j)}_{i}
whenever
j\neq j^{\prime}
. These additional relations are responsible for the new type of length
4
cycles.
∎

2.3.2.
Graph of labelled forests.

A
labelled forest diagram
is simply a forest diagram in
\mathcal{D}^{F}(m)
together with a labeling of its trees by
1,\dots,s
, where
s
is the size of the forest, i.e. a choice of identification
\pi_{0}(F)\cong\{1,\dots,m\}
. We say that two labelled forests
F,F^{\prime}
are related by a
slide move
if
F^{\prime}
is obtained from
F
by permuting adjacent legs belonging to distinct trees:

(2.23)

F=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\;\xleftrightarrow{\text{slide move}}F^{\prime}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}\;,

where it is understood, as usual, that
F
and
F^{\prime}
are identical outside of the part shown.

Definition 2.3.2.1
.

Given
s
-many tree diagrams
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
on
m
strands (
s>0
), the
graph of labelled forests on
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
is the undirected graph whose set of vertices is the set

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})

of all labelled forests on
m
strands, whose
i
-th tree is
T_{i}
, and where two labelled forests are connected by an edge if they are related by a slide move.

A part of a graph of labelled forests, corresponding to what happens on one strand, is shown on Figure
6(b)
. On this figure, the two leftmost legs (e.g. in the bottom diagram) belong to
T_{1}
, while the next ones belong to
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
, respectively
24
24

24

For simplicity, we have labelled only the forest at the bottom, but all forests on the figure should be labelled accordingly.
. The legs from
T_{1}
are not allowed to slide across one another, but they can slide across the two other legs. Note that any two vertices in
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
are connected by
at most
one edge. There are no edges when
s=1
.

Proposition 2.3.2.2
.

Suppose that the tree
T_{i}
has
n^{j}_{i}
legs on the
j
-th strand, for
1\leq i\leq s
and
1\leq j\leq m
. Then there is a graph isomorphism defined on vertices as
25
25

25

Since there is at most one edge connecting two vertices, this definition determines the morphism on edges as well.

(2.24)

W\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Box_{j=1}^{m}P_{\textbf{n}^{j}}

where
\textbf{n}^{j}=(n^{j}_{1},\dots,n^{j}_{s})
, sending a labelled forest to
(w^{j})_{j}
where
w^{j}
encodes the ordering of the legs on the
j
-strand, from top to bottom.

Proof.

This is immediate, since a labelled forest is entirely determined by its trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
and the relative positions of their legs on the strands.
∎

Corollary 2.3.2.3
.

The first homology of the graph of labelled forests
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
is generated by length
4
and
6
cycles. In other words, any
1
-cycle in it is the boundary of a union of squares and hexagons.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Proposition
2.3.2.2
and Corollary
2.3.1.5
.
∎

Definition 2.3.2.4
.

To a directed edge
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
,
s\geq 2
, one can assign the third term appearing in the
\STU
relation, which we denote
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)
:

(2.25)

\text{If }F=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\;\text{ and }F^{\prime}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}\;\text{, then }\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}=\overrightarrow{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}}\coloneqq\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}.

In particular,
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}=F^{\prime}-F
in
\mathcal{A}(m)
.

The fact that the legs involved in a slide move belong to distinct trees is important here, otherwise
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}
would have a cycle and would not be a forest diagram.

There are many paths joining forests
F,F^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
. Hence,
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}
is not well-defined for non-adjacent forests. However, any two paths differ by a cycle in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, which by Corollary
2.3.2.3
can be written as a sum of back-and-forth paths, length
4
and
6
cycles. Thus, modding out the target
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)
by those cycles will give us a well-defined map
\overrightarrow{\cdot}
.

Definition 2.3.2.5
.

A
\square
relation
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)
is a relation of the form

(2.26)

\overrightarrow{F_{1}F_{2}}+\overrightarrow{F_{2}F_{3}}+\overrightarrow{F_{3}F_{4}}+\overrightarrow{F_{4}F_{1}}

where
(F_{1},F_{2},F_{3},F_{4})
is a length
4
cycle in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
for some trees
T_{i}
. See Figure
7(a)
.

Similarly, a
\hexagon

relation
in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)
is a relation of the form

(2.27)

\overrightarrow{F_{1}F_{2}}+\overrightarrow{F_{2}F_{3}}+\overrightarrow{F_{3}F_{4}}+\overrightarrow{F_{4}F_{5}}+\overrightarrow{F_{5}F_{6}}+\overrightarrow{F_{6}F_{1}}

where
(F_{1},\dots,F_{6})
is a length
6
cycle in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
for some trees
T_{i}
. See Figure
7(b)
.

Let
\square R
, resp.
\hexagon R
, denote the submodule of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{F}(m)
generated by all
\square
, resp.
\hexagon
, relations.

Remark 2.3.2.6
.

A
\hexagon
relation can be obtained in the following way: start with an
\IHX
relation that involves a leg, and break apart that leg in the three terms. The resulting signed sum of six terms is an
\hexagon
relation, and all
\hexagon
relations can be obtained in this way.
∎

(a)

A
\square
relation

(b)

A
\hexagon
relation

Figure 7
.
Relations obtained from cycles of length
4
and
6

By the above definition and Corollary
2.3.2.3
, the following definition is well-defined.

Definition 2.3.2.7
.

For two labelled forests
F,F^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
,
s\geq 2
, define

(2.28)

\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}\coloneqq\overrightarrow{FF_{1}}+\dots+\overrightarrow{F_{l-1}F^{\prime}}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.12001pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.33333pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}}

where
F\rightsquigarrow F_{1}\rightsquigarrow\dots\rightsquigarrow F^{\prime}
is a path from
F
to
F^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
.

Any two choices of paths differ by a cycle, which can be decomposed into back-and-forth paths, squares and hexagons, by Corollary
2.3.2.3
. The relations
\AS
,
\square R
and
\hexagon R
respectively take care of these. This shows that the above definition is well-defined.

Proposition 2.3.2.8
.

Square relations and
\STU^{2}
relations coincide, i.e.
\square R=\STU^{2}
as submodules of
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)
.

Proof.

Recall
[
Con08
, Definition 3.1]
that
\STU^{2}
relations among size
s
forests are defined starting with a
template
, i.e. a size
s-1
forest diagram or a size
s
Feynman diagram with a single cycle, then choosing two legs (which must be adjacent to the cycle in the latter case) and breaking one or the other apart using
\STU
. Breaking both apart yields four possible size
s+1
forest diagrams, which form the corners of a square in a graph of labelled forests and the corresponding
\square
relation coincides with the
\STU^{2}
relation.

Conversely, consider a square as on Figure
7(a)
and the associated
\square
relation. Then the template

yields a
\STU^{2}
relation which coincides with that
\square
relation.
∎

2.3.3.
Graph of forests.

In the previous section we investigated the graph of labelled forests and the relations that are required to define differences between labelled forests that are joined by a path. However, forests diagrams in the bialgebra
\mathcal{A}(m)
are not labelled. The
true
graph of forests is a quotient of the graph of labelled forests, where the quotient map forgets the labelling.

Definition 2.3.3.1
.

The
graph of forests
on trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
is the graph
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
whose vertices are all forest diagrams in
\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)
consisting of those trees, and to each slide move relating two forests there is an associated edge joining the corresponding vertices.

Note that
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
does not depend on the ordering of the trees
T_{i}
. If
F=\bigcup_{i=1}^{s}T_{i}
is a forest diagram consisting of trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
, we write
\mathcal{F}(F)\coloneqq\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
.

There is a natural map

(2.29)

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})

that forgets the labels of forests. It is surjective but not injective if there are identical trees among
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
.

Remark 2.3.3.2
.

In
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, it can happen that two vertices are joined by multiple edges. See Figure
8
for an example: the top, resp. bottom, edge corresponds to exchanging the legs on the right, resp. left, strand.

Figure 8
.
Two different leg moves joining two forests

Thus, the notation
\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}
is ambiguous. Given a directed edge
F\overset{e}{\rightsquigarrow}F^{\prime}
we write
\overrightarrow{e}
to denote the third term of the corresponding
\STU
relation, i.e.
\overrightarrow{e}=F^{\prime}-F
in
\mathcal{A}(m)
.
∎

Lemma 2.3.3.3
.

For any labelled forest diagram
F
, the forgetful map
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(F)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(F)
satisfies the following
path-lifting property
: For any path
P=F_{0}\overset{e_{0}}{\rightsquigarrow}F_{1}\overset{e_{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}\dots\overset{e_{l-1}}{\rightsquigarrow}F_{l}
in
\mathcal{F}(F)
and any choice of labelled forest
\tilde{F_{0}}
lifting
F_{0}
, there is a unique path
\tilde{P}=\tilde{F}_{0}\overset{\tilde{e}_{0}}{\rightsquigarrow}\tilde{F}_{1}\overset{\tilde{e}_{1}}{\rightsquigarrow}\dots\overset{\tilde{e}_{l-1}}{\rightsquigarrow}\tilde{F}_{l}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(F)
that lifts
P
. In other words, the surjection
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(F)\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(F)
is a finite covering.

Moreover,
\overrightarrow{\tilde{e}_{i}}=\overrightarrow{e_{i}}
for each
i=0,\dots,l-1
.

Proof.

Starting with
\tilde{F}_{0}
and applying the sequence of slide moves prescribed by
P
yields such a lift
\tilde{P}
, which is uniquely determined by this process.
∎

Remark 2.3.3.4
.

The structure of the first homology
H_{1}(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))
over
\mathbb{Z}
is not as simple as in the labelled case. The resulting new relations will be investigated in future work. The good news is that those problems disappear once we pass to rational coefficients.
∎

Proposition 2.3.3.5
.

Any cycle in
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
has a multiple that lifts along the covering

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}).

In particular, over
\mathbb{Q}
, for any cycle
C
, the element
\overrightarrow{C}
belongs to the submodule
\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle\subset\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)
.

Proof.

Let
G
be the group of deck transformations of the finite covering
p\colon\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, and
\lvert G\rvert
its size. Consider the transfer homomorphism
\tau\colon H_{1}(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))\rightarrow H_{1}(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))
. Then
p_{*}\circ\tau
equals multiplication by
\lvert G\rvert
. In particular, the cokernel of
p_{*}
is
\lvert G\rvert
-torsion and
p_{*}
becomes surjective when passing to coefficients
\mathbb{Q}
. This concludes.

The second statement then follows from the first and Corollary
2.3.2.3
.
∎

With the above result in hands, we can adapt Definition
2.3.2.7
to the graph of forests, but with rational coefficients.

Definition 2.3.3.6
.

For two forests
F,F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, define

(2.30)

\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}\coloneqq\overrightarrow{P}\in{\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.12001pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.33333pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s-1}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\AS,\square R,\hexagon R\rangle}$}}}

where
P
is a path from
F
to
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
.

2.4.
Presentation of the primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

2.4.1.
Splitting trees.

Definition 2.4.1.1
.

For
1\leq s\leq n-1
, the
\mathbb{Q}
-module of size
s
forest diagrams

\mathcal{F}^{s}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is defined by

(2.31)

\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq{\mathchoice{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.75pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.625pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{1.875pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R\rangle}$}}}.

It is graded by the degree of diagrams, with degree
n
graded part denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Remark 2.4.1.2
.

When
s=1
, the submodule
\hexagon R
is actually trivial, since an
\hexagon
relation requires at least two trees. Thus,
\mathcal{F}^{1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathcal{L}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
as
\mathbb{Q}
-modules.

In degree
n
and with
s=n
,
\STU^{2}
becomes
\4T
while
\hexagon R
is trivial, hence we have
\mathcal{F}^{n}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

If
s<n-1
, the
\hexagon
relations are actually implied by
\STU^{2}
and
\IHX
. Indeed, by Remark
2.3.2.6
any
\hexagon
relation can be obtained by breaking apart a leg in a
\IHX
relation. Since
s<n-1
, the terms of the
\hexagon
relation must contain a node not involved in the relation. Apply
\STU^{2}
to break that additional node and rebuild a sum of two
\IHX
relations. Thus
\hexagon R\subset\langle\STU^{2},\IHX\rangle
as submodules of
\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{s}(m)
when
s<n-1
.
∎

For each
1\leq s<n
, there is a linear map

(2.32)

\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}\colon\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{s}(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{n}^{s+1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

defined as follows: given a size
s
forest diagram
F\in\mathcal{D}^{s}_{n}(m)
, pick a trivalent vertex (which must exist since
s<n
) adjacent to a strand and break it apart according to
\STU
, i.e.

(2.33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\right)\coloneqq\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}.

By
\STU^{2}
, this does not depend on the choice of trivalent vertex to break apart.

Proposition 2.4.1.3
.

The map
\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{s}(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(\FI),s+1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
factors through the linear map
\iota^{s}_{n}\colon\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),n+1}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

This proof is very similar to the proof of Claim 3.4 in
[
Con08
]
.

We need to show that
\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}
vanishes on
\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R
. If
s<n-2
, then forest diagrams in
\mathcal{D}_{n}^{s}(m)
have at least three nodes, while the relations
\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R
involve at most
2
nodes, hence it suffices to pick another node to break apart, hence the image under
\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}
is a difference of sums that belong to the respective submodules.

For
s<n-1
, the above argument also applies to
\STU^{2},\hexagon R,\AS
. For
s=n-2
and
\IHX
, observe that an
\IHX
relation is sent to an
\hexagon
relation.

For
s=n-1
, there are no
\IHX
relations, while a direct calculation shows that
\STU^{2}
and
\AS
relations are sent to zero.

This concludes the proof that
\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}
factors through
\mathcal{F}^{s}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The
\FI
case holds as well: if
D
has an isolated chord, then so do the two terms in
\tilde{\iota}^{s}_{n}(D)
.
∎

Remark 2.4.1.4
.

In
[
Con08
, Claim 3.4]
, the proof restricts to the case
s\neq n-1
because the
\hexagon
relation is not taken into account there. This relation appears naturally here by considering graphs of forests and solves that issue.
∎

The linear maps
\iota^{s}_{n}
,
s=1,\dots,n-1
provide a factorization of the map
\iota_{n}\colon\mathcal{F}^{1}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, where nodes are broken apart one after the other, instead of all at once

2.4.2.
Barycenters of forests.

Let
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
be tree diagrams. The set of vertices of the associated graph of forests
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
shares some properties with affine spaces. Recall that an
affine space
can be defined as a principal homogeneous space
A
for the action of the additive group of a vector space
\overrightarrow{A}
, its
direction
. In particular, there is a well-defined map

(2.34)

\overrightarrow{\cdot}\colon A\times A\longrightarrow\overrightarrow{A}\colon(a,b)\longmapsto\overrightarrow{ab}.

Although
\mathcal{F}^{s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
does not act on
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, Definition
2.3.3.6
defines an analogous map

(2.35)

\overrightarrow{\cdot}\colon\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\times\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon(F,F^{\prime})\longmapsto\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}.

Consequently,
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
inherits some properties of affine spaces. In particular, we can still talk about barycenters of vertices of
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
.

Definition 2.4.2.1
.

The
barycenter
of forests
F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\in\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
with coefficients
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
summing to
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=1
is the formal sum
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}F_{i}
.

Denote by
\Bary(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))
the set of all barycenters of forests in
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
. There is a natural inclusion
\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\xhookrightarrow{}\Bary(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))
sending a forest
F
to the formal sum
1\cdot F
.

Lemma 2.4.2.2

(Chasles relation)
.

If
F_{0},F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
, then
\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{2}}=\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{1}}+\overrightarrow{F_{1}F_{2}}
.

Proof.

By Definition
2.3.3.6
, we can choose any path from
F_{0}
to
F_{2}
in order to obtain
\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{2}}
. In particular, choosing a path that passes through
F_{1}
, we get
\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{2}}=\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{1}}+\overrightarrow{F_{1}F_{2}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.4.2.3
.

Let
F_{0},F^{\prime}_{0},F_{1},\dots,F_{k}\in\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})
and
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k}\in\mathbb{Q}
. If
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=0
, then
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{i}}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}_{0}F_{i}}
in
\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

By the Chasles relation (Lemma
2.4.2.2
), we immediately get

\displaystyle{\sum}_{i}\lambda_{i}\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}_{0}F_{i}}=\underbrace{\left({\sum}_{i}\lambda_{i}\right)}_{=0}\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}_{0}F_{0}}+{\sum}_{i}\lambda_{i}\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{i}}.

∎

Definition 2.4.2.4
.

Given two barycenters
B=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}F_{i}
,
B^{\prime}=\sum_{j}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}F^{\prime}_{i}
in
\Bary(\mathcal{F}(F))
, we define the
vector
from
B
to
B^{\prime}
as

(2.36)

\overrightarrow{BB^{\prime}}\coloneqq\sum_{j}\lambda^{\prime}_{j}\overrightarrow{F_{0}F^{\prime}_{j}}-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\overrightarrow{F_{0}F_{i}}

where
F_{0}
is any forest in
\mathcal{F}(F)
.

Proposition 2.4.2.5
.

Definition
2.4.2.4
is well-defined. More precisely, the map

(2.37)

\overrightarrow{\cdot}\colon\Bary(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))\times\Bary(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

extends the map (
2.35
) and the Chasles relation holds for barycenters of forests.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4.2.3
, the value of
\overrightarrow{BB^{\prime}}
does not depend on the choice of origin
F_{0}
.

For barycenters of the form
1\cdot F
and
1\cdot F^{\prime}
, we have
\overrightarrow{(1\cdot F)(1\cdot F^{\prime})}=\overrightarrow{FF^{\prime}}
, e.g. by choosing
F_{0}=F
. Thus the map
\overrightarrow{\cdot}
for barycenters indeed extends that for forests.

For three barycenters
B=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}F_{i}
,
B^{\prime}=\sum_{i}\lambda^{\prime}_{i}F^{\prime}_{i}
and
B^{\prime\prime}=\sum_{i}\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{i}F^{\prime\prime}_{i}
, the Chasles relation
\overrightarrow{BB^{\prime\prime}}=\overrightarrow{BB^{\prime}}+\overrightarrow{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}
follows by writing
\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{i}-\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{\prime\prime}-\lambda_{i}^{\prime}+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}
.
∎

It follows from Proposition
2.4.2.5
that any choice of barycenter
B_{0}\in\Bary(\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s}))
defines a map

(2.38)

\mathcal{F}(T_{1},\dots,T_{s})\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon F\longmapsto\overrightarrow{B_{0}F}

which sends a forest of size
s
to a linear combination of forests of size
s-1
. Maps like the above will be crucial in defining sections to the maps
\iota^{s}_{n}
. Doing so requires picking a barycenter in each graph of forest, but random choices will not work in general. The barycenters must be consistently chosen.

Definition 2.4.2.6
.

For
F
a forest of size
s
consisting of trees
T_{1},\dots,T_{s}
, define the
average barycenter
, or
average basepoint
, associated to
F
as

(2.39)

(F)_{\avg}\coloneqq\frac{1}{s!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Sy_{s}}T_{\sigma(1)}\dots T_{\sigma(s)},

i.e. the average of all possible ways of stacking the trees of
F
one above the other.

Note that
(F)_{\avg}=(F^{\prime})_{\avg}
whenever
F
and
F^{\prime}
consist of the same trees, i.e. they belong to a common graph of forests.

2.4.3.
Merging trees.

Consider the linear map

(2.40)

\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}\colon\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{s}_{n}(m)\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\colon F\longmapsto\overrightarrow{(F)_{\avg}F}

that assigns to a forest
F
the vector from the average
(F)_{\avg}
to
F
. It can be thought of as a sort of distance function from the average barycenters.

Lemma 2.4.3.1

(Diagrammatic
\STU
)
.

For any adjacent forests
F^{=}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}
and
F^{\times}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}
in a graph of forests, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\right)=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}

where
\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}
is an
\STU
relation.

Proof.

Since
F^{=}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}
and
F^{\times}=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}
belong to the same graph of forests, we have
(F^{=})_{\avg}=(F^{\times})_{\avg}
and

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}(F^{=}-F^{\times})=\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}(F^{=})-\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}(F^{\times})=\overrightarrow{(F^{=})_{\avg}F^{=}}-\overrightarrow{(F^{\times})_{\avg}F^{\times}}=\overrightarrow{F^{\times}F^{=}}=F^{Y}

by the Chasles relation.
∎

Proposition 2.4.3.2
.

The map
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}\colon\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{s}(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(\FI),s-1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
factors through
\pi^{s}_{n}\colon\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),s-1}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Proof.

We need to show that
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}
vanishes on
(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}
and
\hexagon R
. The argument is similar to
[
Con08
, Claim 3.6]
.

Start with an
\AS
relation
F+F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{D}^{s}_{n}(m)
. We can write
(F^{(^{\prime})})_{\avg}=\sum_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}F^{(^{\prime})}_{\sigma}
where
F_{\sigma}+F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
are
\AS
relations (the notation
(^{\prime})
indicates that we can read it with or without
′
). Pick a path
F_{\sigma}\overset{e_{0}}{\rightsquigarrow}\dots\overset{e_{l-1}}{\rightsquigarrow}F
of leg slides in
\mathcal{F}(F)
. Then we can follow the parallel path
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}\overset{e^{\prime}_{0}}{\rightsquigarrow}\dots\overset{e^{\prime}_{l-1}}{\rightsquigarrow}F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}(F^{\prime})
, obtained by performing the exact same leg slides. Notice that for each
j
, the origin and extremities of
e_{j}
and
e^{\prime}_{j}
only differ by the cyclic ordering of a node, in the same trees as for
F
and
F^{\prime}
. Consequently,
\overrightarrow{e_{j}}+\overrightarrow{e^{\prime}_{j}}
is an
\AS
relation for each
j
and
\overrightarrow{F_{\sigma}F}+\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}_{\sigma}F^{\prime}}
is a sum of those. In particular,

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{(F)_{\avg}F}+\overrightarrow{(F^{\prime})_{\avg}F^{\prime}}={\sum}_{\sigma}\lambda_{\sigma}(\overrightarrow{F_{\sigma}F}+\overrightarrow{F^{\prime}_{\sigma}F^{\prime}})=0.

For an
\IHX
relation
F^{I}+F^{H}+F^{X}
, the argument is the same. As above, picking parallel paths from
F^{\bullet}_{\sigma}
to
F^{\bullet}
, for
\bullet\in\{I,H,X\}
, we obtain

\overrightarrow{(F^{I})_{\avg}F^{I}}+\overrightarrow{(F^{H})_{\avg}F^{H}}+\overrightarrow{(F^{X})_{\avg}F^{X}}=0

since it is a sum of
\IHX
relations.

Unlike
\AS
and
\IHX
which happen far from the strands,
\STU^{2}
happens close to the strands and we need to distinguish two cases. If the
\STU^{2}
relation
F^{Y=}-F^{Y\times}-F^{=Y}+F^{\times Y}
involves two or three trees, then the legs forming
=
or
\times
must belong to distinct trees
26
26

26

Otherwise, joining them in the term with
Y
would form a cycle.
Then, two applications of the diagrammatic
\STU
(Lemma
2.4.3.1
) yield

\displaystyle\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}(F^{Y=}-F^{Y\times})=F^{YY}=\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}(F^{=Y}-F^{\times Y}),

hence the
\STU^{2}
relation is satisfied by
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}
in this case. Let us turn to the second case, where the
\STU^{2}
relation involves a single tree, i.e. all the legs involved belong to the same tree. We argue as for the
\AS
and
\IHX
relations, by picking four parallel paths of slide moves from
F^{\bullet}_{\sigma}
to
F^{\bullet}
, for
\bullet\in\{Y\!=,Y\times,=\!Y,\times Y\}
. More precisely, call the places involved in the
\STU^{2}
relation
\alpha
and
\beta

\displaystyle\underbrace{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}}_{\alpha}\underbrace{\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}}_{\beta}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\;\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}.

A leg sliding across site
\alpha
induces one or two edges in the four parallel paths, depending on whether site
\beta
contains a “
Y
”, “
=
” or “
\times
” configuration. The corresponding terms in
\sum_{\bullet}\overrightarrow{F^{\bullet}_{\sigma}F^{\bullet}}
add up to zero by
\STU^{2}
and
\hexagon R
:

\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=bottom.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=top.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-xbottom.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-xbottom.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\underbrace{-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y+.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y+.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}}_{\overset{\STU^{2}}{=}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y=.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-YX.eps}}}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}}\overset{\hexagon R}{=}0.

When a leg slides across site
\beta
, the argument is the same. Thus
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}
satisfies
\STU^{2}
.

It remains to show that
\hexagon
-relations are satisfied. This immediately follows from three applications of the diagrammatic
\STU
Lemma (Lemma
2.4.3.1
) and the
\IHX
relation.

This concludes the proof that
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}
factors through
\mathcal{F}^{s}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

In the
\FI
case, we need to check that
\tilde{\pi}^{s}_{n}
vanishes on
\1T
. Let
F\in\mathcal{D}^{s}_{n}(m)
be a forest diagram with an isolated chord. For
\sigma\in\Sy_{s}
, the element
\overrightarrow{(F)_{\sigma}F}
is a sum of diagrams in which this isolated chord remains, together with diagrams coming from sliding that isolated chord across another leg. Those terms of the latter type vanish in pairs by
\AS
. This also follows from the commutativity property, shown in the proof of
[
Bar95
, Lemma 3.1]
.
∎

Proposition 2.4.3.3
.

For each
1\leq s<n
, the map
\pi^{s+1}_{n}
is a section of
\iota^{s}_{n}
, i.e.
\pi^{s+1}_{n}\circ\iota^{s}_{n}=\id_{\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(\FI),s}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}}
. In particular,
\iota^{s}_{n}
is injective.

Proof.

Given a size
s
and degree
n
forest diagram
F=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}
(represented by one of its nodes adjacent to a strand), we have
\iota^{s}_{n}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\right)=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=20.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}
and then

\displaystyle\pi^{s+1}_{n}\circ\iota^{s}_{n}(F)=\pi^{s+1}_{n}\circ\iota^{s}_{n}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}\right)=\pi^{s+1}_{n}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-=.eps}}}-\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-x.eps}}}\right)=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=25.0pt]{Ressources/permutograph/STU2-satisfied-by-s/term-Y.eps}}}=F

by the diagrammatic
\STU
Lemma (Lemma
2.4.3.1
).
∎

2.4.4.
From
\mathcal{F}^{k}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
to
F^{k}\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

Combining the results of Sections
2.2
and
2.4.3
, we obtain the following theorem.

Theorem 2.4.4.1
.

For each
1\leq k\leq n
, there is a natural linear isomorphism

(2.41)

\mathcal{F}_{n}^{(\FI),k}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}F^{k}\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

More precisely, we have the following isomorphism of filtrations (with
\FI
as well):

(2.42)

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

under which the sections
s^{k}_{n}
and
\pi^{k}_{n}
coincide.

Proof.

We omit
\FI
. By definition,
F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is generated by size
k
forests in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, i.e. there is a linear surjection
\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)\twoheadrightarrow F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The relations
\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2},\hexagon R
in
\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{k}(m)
are all consequences of the
\AS,\IHX,\STU
relations that hold in
\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
, hence this surjection descends to a surjection

\mathcal{F}_{n}^{k}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow F^{k}\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

Notice that those surjections, for
1\leq k\leq n
, combine into the vertical maps in (
2.42
) and commute with the maps
i^{k}_{n}
and
\iota^{k}_{n}
. By Proposition
2.4.3.3
, the maps
\iota
are injective, hence so are the vertical maps.

To see that the sections
s^{k}_{n}
and
\pi^{k}_{n}
coincide, compare (
2.18
) with (
2.40
) and Definition
2.4.2.6
of
(\cdot)_{\avg}
.
∎

2.4.5.
The Lie algebra of trees.

From the presentation of the primitive filtration of
\mathcal{A}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
in Theorem
2.4.4.1
, we directly obtain a concrete presentation of the primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
.

Theorem 2.4.5.1
.

The primitive Lie algebra
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
is naturally isomorphic to the
Lie algebra of trees

(2.43)

\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\coloneqq\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle(\1T),\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}}

endowed with the bracket

(2.44)

\begin{split}[,]\colon\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}&\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\\
(T,T^{\prime})&\longmapsto[T,T^{\prime}]\coloneqq\overrightarrow{(T^{\prime}\cdot T)(T\cdot T^{\prime})}\end{split}

for any
T,T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
and extended linearly.

Proof.

Under the isomorphism of filtrations from Theorem
2.4.4.1
and by Theorem
2.2.2.3
, we have an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}
-modules (we omit
(\FI)
)

\mathcal{L}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong F^{1}\mathcal{A}_{\geq 1}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})

and
\overrightarrow{(T^{\prime}\cdot T)(T\cdot T^{\prime})}
in
\mathcal{L}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
corresponds to
T\cdot T^{\prime}-T^{\prime}\cdot T
in
Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
by definition of
\overrightarrow{(\cdot)}
, hence
\mathcal{L}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\cong\Prim(\mathcal{A}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})
as Lie algebras as well. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.4.5.2
.

The Lie algebra of trees
\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is graded by the degree of tree diagrams, with degree
n
graded part
\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathcal{F}^{(\FI),1}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. By construction, the Lie bracket is compatible with the grading:

[\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}_{n}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}},\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}_{n^{\prime}}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}]\subset\mathcal{L}^{(\FI)}_{n+n^{\prime}}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}.

∎

3.
The rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links

3.1.
Clasper calculus and realization of diagrams

In the previous section, we identified the rational primitive Lie algebra of tree diagrams
\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. It is given by concrete relations on tree diagrams, hence we can check that the
clasper surgery realization

R\colon\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}(m)\rightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
satisfies those relations, so that it factors through
\Prim(\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}})\rightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. In other words, we check that the diagrammatic relations
\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}
are satisfied geometrically in the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra. Most of the argument is adapted from known clasper manipulations from Habiro
[
Hab00
]
, Goussarov
[
Gou98
]
, Ohtsuki
[
Oht02
]
, Conant–Teichner
[
CT04a
,
CT04
]
and Kosanovic
[
Kos20
]
. In particular, we provide a detailed proof of the
\IHX
relation, different from the ones in the aforementioned references.

The definitions of claspers and clasper surgeries are not recalled in details. We refer the reader to the original papers of Habiro
[
Hab00
]
and Goussarov
[
Gou98
,
GPV00
]
.

3.1.1.
Claspers.

A
simple tree clasper

C
on a string link
\gamma
is a ribbon uni-trivalent tree diagram embedded in the complement of
\gamma
, with
leaves
attached to the strands. See Figure
9
, on the left, where
\gamma
is the trival string link on three strands. We use Habiro’s drawing convention
[
Hab00
, Figure 7]
: nodes (tri-valent vertices) are dots, leaves (uni)valent vertices) are disks clasping around a strand and ribbon edges have the blackboard framing with half-twists indicated by small barred disks.

Figure 9
.
A degree
3
simple tree clasper and the resulting clasper surgery.

A tree clasper
C
has a degree
\deg(C)=\#\text{leaves}-1
, which is equal to the degree of its underlying tree diagram. For example, the tree claspers in Figure
4
have degree
1
and
2
, respectively, while the one in Figure
9
has degree
3
.

In
[
Hab00
]
, Habiro defines more general claspers than just simple tree claspers. Those naturally appear when manipulating tree claspers. A clasper
C
determines a link
L_{C}
. Dehn surgery along
L_{C}
is called
clasper surgery along
C
. A clasper surgery does not modify the ambient manifold
27
27

27

In general, this is the case when the clasper is
tame
, which is always the case in our context. See
[
Hab00
]
for more details.
(here, the cylinder), but does modify the string link. The result of clasper surgery along a clasper
C
on
\gamma
is denoted
\gamma^{C}
. When
\gamma
is the trivial string link
\gamma_{0}=1
, we write
\sigma(C)\coloneqq\gamma_{0}=1^{C}
. See Figures
4
and
9
for examples of clasper surgeries.

Habiro’s clasper calculus
consists in a collection of
moves
, i.e. modifications on claspers that do not alter the resulting surgery
\gamma^{C}
, those moves are pictured in
[
Hab00
, Proposition 2.7]
, from move 1 to move 12. We will refer to Habiro’s moves by the same numbers and sometimes write, e.g.
H7
for move 7.

Observe that clasper surgeries are entirely supported in a regular neighborhood of the clasper, hence they define
local
operations on the set of string links and naturally lead to the study of equivalence classes of string links under those moves. Different filtrations or degrees on the set of claspers define various equivalence relations.

3.1.2.
The Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links.

In this paper, we stick to the usual degree for trees, inducing local moves known as
C_{n}
-moves
, for
n\geq 1
. Let
\gamma
be a string link. A
C_{n}
-move
on
\gamma
is a surgery along a degree
n
tree clasper on
\gamma
. Two string links
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}
are said to be
C_{n}
-equivalent
, denoted
\gamma\overset{C_{n}}{\sim}\gamma^{\prime}
, if one can be obtained from the other by applying a sequence of
C_{n}
-moves. The
C_{n}
-equivalence relation is symmetric by
[
Hab00
, Proposition 3.23]
. This defines the Goussarov–Habiro filtration of the monoid of string links
L(m)

(3.1)

L(m)=L_{1}(m)\supset L_{2}(m)\supset L_{3}(m)\supset\dots

where
L_{n}(m)\coloneqq\{\gamma\in L(m)\mid\gamma\overset{C_{n}}{\sim}1\}
is the submonoid of
C_{n}
-trivial string links, i.e. string links that are
C_{n}
-equivalent to the trivial string link
1
. The quotient monoids
L_{k}(m)/C_{n+1}
are finitely generated nilpotent groups
[
Hab00
, Theorem 5.4]
. They are abelian groups when
k=n
, in which case they are denoted

(3.2)

\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)\coloneqq\overline{L_{n}(m)}\coloneqq L_{n}(m)/C_{n+1},

and more generally, for any
1\leq k,k^{\prime}\leq n
, one has

(3.3)

\left[L_{k}(m)/{C_{n+1}},L_{k^{\prime}}(m)/{C_{n+1}}\right]\subset L_{k+k^{\prime}}(m)/{C_{n+1}}.

Thus the abelian groups (
3.2
) combine into the
Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra of string links on
m
strands

(3.4)

\mathcal{L}L(m)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{n\geq 1}\overline{L_{n}(m)},

whose Lie bracket is induced from (
3.3
). Alternatively, the truncation
\mathcal{L}_{\leq n}L(m)
is the graded Lie algebra associated to the
N
-series
\{L_{k}(m)/C_{n+1}\}_{1\leq k\leq n}
of the group
L(m)/{C_{n+1}}
. See
[
Laz54
, Theorem 2.1]
and
[
Mas07
]
. By construction The Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra is graded, and the Lie bracket satisfies the grading by (
3.3
).

Remark 3.1.2.1
.

When
m=1
, the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra
\mathcal{L}L(1)
is commutative, i.e. its bracket is trivial. This follows from the fact that
L(1)
is a commutative monoid or, equivalently, that connected sum of knots is commutative.
∎

3.1.3.
Common moves and zip construction.

Combining Habiro’s moves, one can obtain useful moves that we describe here: edge- and strand-crossing, sliding and twisting moves, and the zip construction.

(a)

(b)

Crossing a strand

(c)

Sliding leaves

(d)

Self-crossing

(e)

Twisting edges

Figure 10
.
Crossing edges and sliding leaves

First, given two trees of respective degrees
p
and
q
in a clasper forests, one can perform a crossing change between edges of those two trees, up to a
C_{p+q+1}
-move, see Figure
10(a)
. Similarly, given a tree of degree
p
, one can perform a crossing change between one of its edges and a tangle strand, up to a
C_{p+1}
-move, see Figure
10(b)
. Lastly, one can slide two leaves, that belong to distinct trees of respective degrees
p
and
q
, across one another, up to a
C_{p+q}
-move, see
10(c)
. See
[
Hab00
, §4]
for proofs. From the moves depicted on Figure
10
(a,b,c) one can deduce two additional useful moves, depicted on Figure
10
(d,e), where the two blue lines in (d) belong to the same tree clasper.

The zip construction allows to get rid of boxes that may appear after some moves. One can imagine the box as a
zipper
that will double everything when unzipped. See
[
Hab00
, §3.3]
for the construction. In Habiro’s version of the zip construction, many new
annoying
boxes appear during the process. If the starting tree has degree
n
, one can get rid of those annoying boxes modulo
C_{n+1}
-moves.

There is also a
box-free
version of the zip construction
28
28

28

See also my master’s thesis, available on my website, for more details and examples of the box-free zip construction.
, free of annoying boxes, detailed by Goussarov
[
Gou01
]
and by Conant–Teichner
[
CT04a
, §4.2]
, based on the move described in
[
CT04a
, Lemma 32]
. Even though the box-free zip construction has no annoying boxes, it produces intricate linking of the resulting tree claspers. One can still get rid of those (modulo
C_{n+1}
) by applying the moves depicted on Figure
10
.

3.1.4.
Realizing tree diagrams in the Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra

We are now able to describe the realization map from tree diagrams to string links. Similar realization maps were considered by Ohtsuki in
[
Oht02
]
, Conant–Teichner
[
CT04a
,
CT04
]
and Kosanovic
[
Kos20
]
,.

Theorem 3.1.4.1
.

For each
n\geq 1
, there is a surjective
\mathbb{Q}
-linear morphism

(3.5)

R_{n}\colon\mathcal{L}^{\FI}_{n}(m)={\mathchoice{\raisebox{4.45613pt}{$\displaystyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.32pt}{$\displaystyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{4.20613pt}{$\textstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-4.07pt}{$\textstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{3.21724pt}{$\scriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}{\raisebox{2.43056pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\mathbb{Q}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)}$}\mkern-5.0mu\diagup\mkern-4.0mu\raisebox{-3.75pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\langle\1T,\AS,\IHX,\STU^{2}\rangle}$}}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{L}_{n}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

that sends a tree diagram
T
to the class of the string link obtained by performing a clasper surgery along a clasper
C
whose underlying diagram
29
29

29

What is meant by
underlying diagram
is explicited in the proof.
is
T
. Those morphisms combine into a surjective morphism of Lie algebras over
\mathbb{Q}

(3.6)

R\colon\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}

from the primitive Lie algebra of trees (Theorem
2.4.5.1
) onto the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra.

Proof.

Definition of the realization map:
First, we define the realization map

\tilde{R}\colon\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)\rightarrow\overline{L_{n}}(m)

as follows: start with a tree diagram
T\in\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)
as on Figure
11(a)
. Since
T
is a tree, its underlying graph is planar and we can pick a planar projection where the cyclic orderings of the nodes are counterclockise. Place that planar projection of the tree to the left of the
m
vertical strands, with the univalent vertices lying on a vertical line parallel to the strands, as on Figure
11(b)
. Attach the leaves of the tree to their respective positions on the strand, as given by the diagram
T
, with an additional
positive
half-twist added to a single chosen leaf
30
30

30

The addition of that additional half-twist is related to the footnote on page 68 in
[
Hab00
]
. Although it is not crucial in the case of realizing trees, it will be when realizing forests later on, in order for the
\STU
relation to hold with the usual signs.
(see Figure
11(c)
). Outside of that additional half-twist, the attachment must be made without introducing half-twists, always going from left to right, and the disk-leaves must clasp to the tangle strands as on Figure
11(c)
. This yields a simple tree clasper
C_{T}
on
m
strands.

(a)

Diagram
T

(b)

Planar tree

(c)

Clasper
C_{T}

(d)

Changing the order

Figure 11
.
Realizing a tree diagram

The only choices that are made in the above process are:

(1)

The choice of ordering of the univalent vertices on the vertical line in Figure
11(c)
. Perturbing this order cyclically introduces full twists, which do not matter modulo
C_{n+1}
-moves by (6) in the proof of
[
Hab00
, Theorem 4.3]
. See Figure
11(d)
.

(2)

The choice of embedding of the edges joining the planar graph to the disk-leaves. Any two such embeddings are related by a sequence of crossing changes of pairs of edges or of edges with tangle strands, which do not matter modulo
C_{n+1}
-moves by Section
3.1.3
.

(3)

The choice of leaf to which we added the additional half-twist. A change of chosen leaf can be obtained by introducing two new half-twists: one to cancel out with the former one, and one as the new one. Since adding a half-twist corresponds to inversing modulo
C_{n+1}
and inversing twice does not change anything, this choice does not matter modulo
C_{n+1}
.

For a tree clasper
C_{T}
in such a position, we say that its
underlying diagram
is
D(C_{T})\coloneqq T
.

Define
\tilde{R}(T)\coloneqq\gamma_{0}^{C_{T}}=\sigma(C_{T})
. Since
C_{T}
is a degree
n
tree clasper, the
C_{n+1}
-equivalence class of
\tilde{R}(T)
is indeed an element of
\overline{L_{n}}(m)
.

Since
\overline{L_{n}}(m)
is an abelian group, the monoid morphism
\tilde{R}
induces a morphism of abelian groups

(3.7)

\tilde{R}\colon\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}_{n}^{T}(m)\rightarrow\overline{L_{n}}(m).

The realization map
\tilde{R}
is surjective:

By
[
Hab00
, Lemma 5.5]
, the abelian group
\overline{L_{n}}(m)
is generated by string links of the form
\sigma(T)
where
T
is a simple tree clasper of degree
n
on
\gamma_{0}
. Let
T
be a simple tree clasper of degree
n
. Pull the nodes to the left of the strands, as on Figure
11
(c). This may require strand-crossing and self-crossing moves, which do not change the class
[T]\in C_{n}(m)
. Flip the nodes so that their cyclic ordering is counterclockwise, and remove half-twists by pairs (again, this does not change the
C_{n+1}
-equivalence class of
T
), leaving either one or zero half-twists. The resulting representative of
[T]_{C_{n+1}}
is then in the image of
\tilde{R}
. More precisely, it is given by
\tilde{R}(D(T))
where
D(T)
is its underlying tree diagram. This shows that
\tilde{R}
is surjective.

The map
\tilde{R}
satisfies
\1T
:
First, the
\1T
relation in
\mathbb{Z}\mathcal{D}^{T}(m)
is trivial except in degree
1
. In degree
1
, the
\1T
relation is shown on Figure
12
. Note that choosing a negative half-twist instead of a positive half-twist results in a
trefoil lonk knot
, which is
C_{2}
-equivalent to the trivial long knot.

Figure 12
.
The
\1T
relation for claspers

The map
\tilde{R}
satisfies
\AS
:
Flipping a node introduces three half-twists, hence we are done since each half-twist corresponds to inversing (see Theorem 4.7 in
[
Hab00
]
):

(3.8)

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.5}]{Ressources/claspers/Habiro_realization/AS_satisfied.eps}}}.

The map
\tilde{R}
satisfies
\IHX
:
Proving that the
\IHX
relation is satisfied requires a bit more care. A proof is given in
[
Gou01
, Theorem 6.6]
in the different but equivalent language of
variations
. A sketch of proof is given in
[
Oht02
, Lemma E.11]
and a proof in four dimensions is given in
[
CST07
]
. We give here a detailed argument.

We will need the
C_{n+1}
-equivalence

(3.9)

which starts with a blue tree clasper of degree
d\geq 1
and a red clasper, which is a tree outside of the leaf depicted, of degree
n-d
. We apply Habiro’s move 12, then the zip construction modulo
C_{n+1}
-moves to obtain two tree claspers
T^{b}
and
T^{r}
, where
T^{b}
(blue) is identical to the original blue tree, and
T^{r}
(red) equals the union of those, with a node instead of the leaf and an additional half-twist. Note that
\deg(T^{r})=n-d+d=n
. Then we can vertically separate
T^{b}
and
T^{r}
modulo
C_{n+d}
-moves, using sliding and edge-crossing moves (see Section
3.1.3
). Since
n+d\geq n+1
, we obtain the desired
C_{n+1}
-equivalence. Note that
T^{b}
and
T^{r}
commute.

With this move in hands, we can prove that the
\IHX
relation holds in
\overline{L_{n}}(m)
:

(3.10)

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.65}]{Ressources/claspers/Habiro_realization/IHX_proof.eps}}}.

The first line of moves follows (
3.9
). The
C_{n+1}
-equivalence marked with
(\ast)
is the subtler step. Before
(\ast)
, all the annoying boxes that have appeared during the zip construction are clasped to the red clasper, which is a tree clasper of degree
n
. Following the box-free zip construction (Section
3.1.3
), we can get rid of those annoying boxes at the price of linking pieces of the red clasper in a complicated way around the blue and green claspers. Then apply the move from Figure (
3.9
) again to the green and blue claspers. Now that it is done, we can bring back the complicated pieces of the red clasper to their original positions, modulo
C_{n+1}
-equivalence. Finally, after vertically separating the trees, we obtain a
product
of the blue, red and green trees. Again, this product is commutative since they have respective degrees
d,n
and
n
.

At the beginning of (
3.10
), we could have directly applied (
3.9
) instead of sliding the leaf down. Comparing the two
C_{n+1}
-equivalences, and cancelling the blue tree (by multiplying both sides by an inverse of that clasper modulo
C_{n+1}
), we obtain the
C_{n+1}
-equivalence

(3.11)

\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[scale={1.7}]{Ressources/claspers/Habiro_realization/IHX_conclusion.eps}}}.

which yields the
\IHX
relation modulo
C_{n+1}
, after removing the half-twists, i.e. taking the inverses of each side.

The map
\tilde{R}
satisfies
\STU^{2}
:
A proof in the case of knots is given in
[
Kos20
, Sec. 10.3]
. We detail here a very similar argument.

The first
C_{n+1}
-equivalence that we need is

(3.12)

starting with a simple clasper
C
with
n
nodes and exactly one cycle. The cycle contains the depicted nodes. First, apply move 10’ (which is move 10 in a slightly different form) and the zip construction to get a tree clasper
T
and a red clasper
C^{\prime}
. Remove the boxes, at the cost of replacing
T
by
T^{\prime}
, which has pieces that link in a neighborhood of the red clasper (those pieces are indicated with a red bar, there are situated where the annoying boxes used to clasp). Next, performing the surgery along the red clasper yields a new tree clasper
T^{\prime\prime}
, which gets an additional small red bar. Up to
C_{n+1}
-equivalence, we can bring back all those pieces of
T^{\prime\prime}
to their original positions, to get
T
again.

The
C_{n+1}
-equivalence depicted below is a form of
\STU
relation for tree claspers
[
Oht02
, Lemma E.11]

(3.13)

where the left-hand-side depicts a non-simple tree clasper of degree
n
, with one leaf clasping around one of its legs. First, slide down the leaf to replace it with two opposing leaves by move 7. Then the zip construction modulo
C_{n+1}
yields two simple tree claspers
T_{=}
and
T^{\prime}_{\times}
. We can separate them, and remove the half-twist of
T_{\times}^{\prime}
up to a minus sign (we use the additive notation since we are working in the abelian group
\overline{L_{n}}(m)
).

Combining those
C_{n+1}
-equivalences, we can finally prove the
\STU^{2}
relation for string links

(3.14)

where the left-hand-side is a clasper (with
n
nodes) with exactly one cycle which contains the two nodes depicted. Add a half-twist anywhere (this is just to avoid removing half-twists at the end). We can apply (
3.13
) to either one of the depicted nodes, hence replacing the node by a leaf together with a half-twist, which cancels out with the original half-twist modulo
C_{n+1}
. Then applying (
3.14
) to the two different outputs yields the desired
\STU^{2}
relation.

This concludes the proof that
\tilde{R}
factors through
R_{n}
as in the statement of this theorem. Those combine into a surjective
\mathbb{Q}
-linear morphism
R\colon\mathcal{L}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}L(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

The map
R
is compatible with the Lie brackets:
Pick two tree diagrams
T,T^{\prime}
of respective degrees
k,l
, let
n\coloneqq k+l
. Denote by the same letters tree claspers realizing them.

Figure 13
.
Commutator of string links (top) versus commutator of tree diagrams (bottom)

The commutator
31
31

31

We omit
\cdot
when writing products: we write
TT^{\prime}
for
T\cdot T^{\prime}
.

[R(T),R(T^{\prime})]
is equal to the
C_{n+1}
-equivalence class of
\sigma(\overline{T}\overline{T^{\prime}}TT^{\prime})
where
\overline{T}
represents the inverse of
T
, idem for
T^{\prime}
, the product is concatenation of tree claspers and
\sigma
denotes surgery modulo
C_{n+1}
-equivalence. The strategy is to go from the clasper
\overline{T}\overline{T^{\prime}}TT^{\prime}
to the clasper
\overline{T}\overline{T^{\prime}}T^{\prime}T
by a sequence
\epsilon_{0},\dots,\epsilon_{r-1}
of slide moves (Section
3.1.3
), see Figure
13
(top). On one hand,
\overline{T}\overline{T^{\prime}}T^{\prime}T\overset{C_{n+1}}{\sim}1
. On the other hand, each slide move creates a red tree
\overrightarrow{\epsilon_{i}}
. We can separate those new red tree claspers since they have degree
n
, hence they commute with all the rest. All in all, we obtain

(3.15)

[\sigma(T),\sigma(T^{\prime})]=\sigma(\overline{T}\overline{T^{\prime}}TT^{\prime})={\sum}_{i=1}^{r-1}\sigma(\overrightarrow{\epsilon_{i}}).

Let us compare (
3.15
) with the commutator of the original tree diagrams
T,T^{\prime}
. We can follow the exact same path of slide moves on the diagrammatic level to get

(3.16)

[T^{\prime},T]=\overrightarrow{(TT^{\prime})(T^{\prime}T)}={\sum}_{i=1}^{r-1}\overrightarrow{e_{i}}.

by Definition
2.3.2.7
.

At this point, one may think that there is a mistake. Indeed, in (
3.15
) and (
3.16
) the roles of
T
and
T^{\prime}
are reversed. The answer is given by the mysterious sign in the definition of the realization map. More precisely, the tree claspers realizing
T,T^{\prime}
both have an additional half-twist introduced somewhere. Thus, the red tree clasper
\overrightarrow{\epsilon_{i}}
has two half-twists, or zero, modulo
C_{n+1}
. This means that
R(\overrightarrow{e_{i}})=-\sigma(\overrightarrow{\epsilon_{i}})
and therefore

(3.17)

R([T,T^{\prime}])=-R([T^{\prime},T])=-{\sum}_{i}R(\overrightarrow{e_{i}})={\sum}_{i}\sigma(\overrightarrow{\epsilon_{i}})=[R(T),R(T^{\prime})],

which concludes the proof that
R
is a surjective morphism of Lie algebras over
\mathbb{Q}
.
∎

Remark 3.1.4.2
.

The above proof already produces a surjective morphism of Lie algebras over
\mathbb{Z}
. We plan on investigating the story over
\mathbb{Z}
in future work.
∎

3.2.
The Kontsevich integral and the tree preservation theorem

The
Kontsevich integral
is the last ingredient of our identification of the rational Goussarov–Habiro Lie algebra. Discovered by Kontsevich
[
Kon93
]
as a universal Vassiliev invariant over
\mathbb{Q}
, it is a morphism

Z\colon L(m)\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}},

which to a string link
\gamma\in L(m)
associates an element
Z(\gamma)
in the graded completion
\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\FI}(m)_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The Kontsevich integral of a string link
Z(\gamma)
can be thought of as a
series expansion
of
\gamma
, such that degree
\leq n
Vassiliev invariants only depend on the degree
\leq n
part
Z_{\leq n}(\gamma)
. However, it is an open question whether
Z
is injective on
L(m)
, i.e. whether the Kontsevich integral classifies string links.

Since the Kontsevich integral turns string links into diagrams, it is a good candidate for inducing an inverse to the realization map
R
.

3.2.1.
The combinatorial Kontsevich integral.

This section gathers results from Bar-Natan
[
Bar95
,
Bar96
]
and Kontsevich
[
Kon93
]
, Le and Murakami
[
LM96
]
as well as the book by Chmutov, Duzhin and Mostovoy
[
CDM12
]
. See also the nice expository papers
[
BS21
,
CD05
]
.

This section gathers the main properties of the combinatorial version of the Kontsevich integral
[
Bar96
]
, to which we stick from now on. The combinatorial Kontsevich integral is understood as a functor whose domain is the category
\PTangles
of
parenthesized tangles
up to isotopy (denoted
PT
in
[
Bar96
]
), i.e. isotopy classes of unframed tangles with a choice of parenthesizing of their endpoints, also referred to as
q-tangles

[
LM96
]
. Its target is the category
\widehat{\Diag}_{\mathbb{Q}}
of uni-trivalent diagrams
[
Bar96
, Def. 3.1]
on tangle skeletons, whose objects are simply
\updownarrow
-words and morphisms are formal series in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(S)_{\mathbb{Q}}
for the adequate tangle skeleton
S
. Composition in
\PTangles
is simply vertical concatenation. Composition in
\widehat{\Diag}_{\mathbb{Q}}
is given by concatenation of the tangle skeleta and concatenation of the diagrams (extended linearly).

Theorem 3.2.1.1

(
[
Bar96
,
CD05
,
Dri91
]
)
.

The Kontsevich integral of unframed parenthesized tangles

(3.18)

Z\colon\PTangles\longrightarrow\widehat{\Diag}_{\mathbb{Q}}

has the following properties:

(i)

It is a
functor
: If
\gamma=\id_{w}
for some parenthesized
\updownarrow
-word
w
, i.e. it consists of parallel vertical strands, then
Z(\gamma)=1\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(m)
is the empty diagram. If
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}
are two concatenable tangles (i.e. composable morphisms in
\PTangles
), then
Z(\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2})=Z(\gamma_{1})\circ Z(\gamma_{2})
.

(ii)

It is a
monoidal
functor, where the monoidal structure on both sides is given by juxtaposition, i.e. horizontal concatenation
32
32

32

In
\PTangles
, the parenthesizing of the tensor product is given by:
u\otimes v\coloneqq((u)(v))
.
:
Z(\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2})=Z(\gamma_{1})\otimes Z(\gamma_{2})
.

(iii)

It takes the following values on the standard
braiding
,
cup
and
cap
parenthesized tangles:

\displaystyle\PTangles(\uparrow\uparrow,\uparrow\uparrow)\ni\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/positive_braiding.eps}}}\quad

\displaystyle\overset{Z}{\longmapsto}\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/braiding_skeleton.eps}}}+\frac{1}{2}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/braiding_skeleton_1_chord.eps}}}+\frac{1}{8}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/braiding_skeleton_2_chords.eps}}}+\dots

(3.19)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/diagram_cross_empty.eps}}}\circ\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/diagram_1_chord.eps}}}\right),

(3.20)

\displaystyle\PTangles(\uparrow\uparrow,\uparrow\uparrow)\ni\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/negative_braiding.eps}}}\quad

\displaystyle\overset{Z}{\longmapsto}\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/diagram_cross_empty.eps}}}\circ\exp\left(\frac{-1}{2}\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/diagram_1_chord.eps}}}\right),

(3.21)

\displaystyle\PTangles(\uparrow\downarrow,\emptyset)\ni\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/cap.eps}}}\quad

\displaystyle\overset{Z}{\longmapsto}\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/cap_skeleton.eps}}},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\PTangles(\emptyset,\downarrow\uparrow)\ni\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/cup.eps}}}\quad

\displaystyle\overset{Z}{\longmapsto}\quad\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/cup_skeleton.eps}}}.

(iv)

It is compatible with the operation of
reversing a strand
. If
\textbf{R}_{C}\gamma
is obtained from
\gamma
by reversing the orientation of the component
C
, then

Z(\textbf{R}_{C}\gamma)=(\textbf{R}_{C})_{*}Z(\gamma)

where
(R_{C})_{*}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(S)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(\textbf{R}_{C}S)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is defined as follows. For a diagram
D
on the tangle skeleton
S=S(\gamma)
,

(\textbf{R}_{C})_{*}(D)=(-1)^{\#\{\text{endpoints on }C\}}D^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(\textbf{R}_{C}S)_{\mathbb{Q}}

where
D^{\prime}
is the same diagram as
D
on the tangle skeleton
\textbf{R}_{C}S=S(\textbf{R}_{C}\gamma)
.

(v)

It is compatible with the operation of
doubling a strand
. If
\textbf{D}_{C}\gamma
is obtained from
\gamma
by doubling

33

33

If
\uparrow

C

\updownarrow

(\uparrow\uparrow)
in
\textbf{D}_{C}\gamma
, and similarly for
\downarrow

the strand
C
, then

Z(\textbf{D}_{C}\gamma)=(\textbf{D}_{C})_{*}Z(\gamma)

where
(D_{C})_{*}\colon\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(S)_{\mathbb{Q}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(\textbf{D}_{C}S)_{\mathbb{Q}}
is defined as follows. For a diagram

on the tangle skeleton

,

(\textbf{D}_{C})_{*}(D)=\sum_{D^{\prime}\text{ lifts }D}D^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}^{\FI}}(\textbf{R}_{C}S)

\displaystyle(\textbf{D}_{3})_{*}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/to_lift.eps}}}\right)=\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/lift_ll.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/lift_lr.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/lift_rl.eps}}}+\vbox{\hbox{\includegraphics[height=30.0pt]{Ressources/Kontsevich_integral/basic_tangles/lift_rr.eps}}},

As explained in
[

, any parenthesized tangle can be decomposed into a product of tensor products of basic tangles. Those basic tangles are the standard tangles from Theorem

It should be mentioned that the construction of a combinatorial Kontsevich integral

, or images of them under a sequence of reversing and doubling operations. The value of the Kontsevich integral

Denote by
